# Arch Browser Development Plan

This plan gives the context needed to continue work in another session, on another Mac, or with a less capable model. Progress lives only here and in `docs/features.md`; there is no external context store.

## Goals

- Build a sidebar-first macOS browser with its own implementation. `docs/features.md` lists every planned feature and its status.
- Run no servers of our own. Use alternatives such as iCloud for features that need servers, including sync (`docs/features.md`, sections 5 and 8).
- Give both engines the same experience. Context menus, dialogs, sheets, find, downloads, extensions, and tools use the app's own interface and work alike whatever a tab's engine; only engine-drawn surfaces such as popups may differ. `docs/features.md` notes any feature an engine cannot support and why.
- Keep features lightweight. Delegate autofill to Apple Passwords or 1Password. Features use macOS facilities by default on both engines to fit macOS as closely as possible. Follow the Platform integration table in `docs/features.md`.
- Prefer public APIs. Use private APIs only behind flags on `flags://`. Check each API's availability quickly at runtime (see Decisions).
- Offer AI features through one AI interface. The user connects providers they subscribe to with an API key or a command-line tool in the built-in terminal. They choose a provider and model for each feature. Arch ships no model or provider account.
- Use ARCH prefixes throughout: `ARCH*` for packages, `Arch` for the app module, and `ARCHKit` for the core layer.
- The browser is for internal use. No distribution is planned. Manage replacement names, images, fonts, and similar items outside this repository.

## Approach

- Write new code for each feature. Start from a row in `docs/features.md`. Design the smallest module that delivers the feature and add it to `Package.swift`.
- Design type names, module boundaries, method signatures, and file layout for Arch. A row in `docs/features.md` describes what a feature does, not how to build it.
- Add engine protocol members only when a feature needs them. The current protocols are a starting point, not a list of requirements to implement.
- Build AI features on the AI interface and plugin system. This lets built-in features, hooks, and features that AI adds at run time use the same calls.
- The sidebar holds navigation, the address field, favorites, and tabs grouped by space. The window background shows the current space's tint. Nothing sits above the page. Only the code is written anew.
- Use the system framework whenever macOS provides the user experience. Do not build or retain an engine-specific version; for example, picture in picture on Chromium uses AVKit. Keep Chromium's existing native integrations. Route the remaining engine events to these frameworks and disable the engine's equivalent user interface.

## Current State

### Engine abstraction

`ARCHEngine` holds the protocols features use, and both adapters implement every member. Every member has a caller in feature code or is a report that an adapter makes, except `postPageScriptMessage(_:toWorld:)`, which the page members of the plugins' context will call (V.11; Decisions); the agents' page script calls `executeJavaScript(_:inWorld:completion:)` (Agent module), and the optional `input`, which the agents' action tools will call (VII.21). An application makes a profile for a storage identifier, nil for the engine's default storage, and a browser for a profile; it reports renderer crashes to its delegate. `Engine.capabilities` names `extensions` when an adapter runs web extensions; the session reads it before starting an engine for them. Both adapters route page events through `EngineWebContentsUIDelegate`; feature code implements only the callbacks it handles, and the defaults give the safe answer (dialogs dismiss, file choosers get no file, permissions deny). A page's file chooser reaches the UI delegate on both adapters as an `EngineFileChooserRequest` (`webContents(_:runFileChooser:completion:)`): one file, several files, a folder, or, only on Chromium, a place to save, with the page's accepted types (lowercase MIME types and dotted extensions), a suggested name for saving, and who asks: the asking frame's origin, which only WebKit names, and the origin of the page's committed main frame address. Neither engine shows a panel of its own for a tab's chooser. The page receives only file addresses, and only the first for a chooser of one item (`answer(from:)`); nil or nothing is a cancel. `allowedContentTypes` maps the accepted types to file types, or to none, so the panel offers every file, when one has no declared type. An adapter that cannot load a main frame navigation in a web contents hands its UI delegate a replacement that loads it (`webContents(_:didCreateReplacement:)`), which the session puts in the tab's place; only the WebKit adapter does, for web extensions' pages. `ScriptMediaSession` gives both adapters one media session, and `EngineCaptureAccess` one camera and microphone check: a site's request for a device the system denies the app is denied without asking. When the user grants a request, the check asks for the system's permission before the page gets the device. It follows the media each document's script reports and shows the playing document, or otherwise the last to report. Between reports, it advances the position at the playback rate. `FaviconLoader` loads both adapters' site icons outside every profile's store, without cookies or stored credentials, so an icon request carries no Persona's sign-in and no cookie set by a response reaches any page. An icon a site shows only to signed-in users does not load. Because a page names its icon's URL, `FaviconLoader` loads only web and data URLs and drops a body past 1 MB, also one without a stated length, and a download not finished after 30 seconds. Both adapters report a link the user clicks with Shift and without Command to `EngineWebContentsNavigationDelegate` as a user-initiated `newWindow` action; the WebKit adapter asks the navigation delegate about every navigation, the Chromium adapter only about these links. `EngineWebContents.readableContent(limit:completion:)` returns the main frame's visible text (headings as `#` lines, list items as `- ` lines, paragraphs separated by blank lines), its address, title, `lang`, and the selection outside fields and editable areas. Both adapters run the same script, `EngineReadableContent.script`, which skips scripts, styles, templates, hidden and `aria-hidden` subtrees, the content of closed `details`, field values, and editable regions, follows open shadow roots and their slots, and stops at the limit. `EngineWebContents.input` (`EngineInput`, Tier 2) moves the pointer, clicks, turns the wheel, presses keys, and inserts text as the user's own input, which the page sees with `isTrusted` true, at points in the page's view; each call sends all of its events or throws `EngineInputError` and sends none, and both adapters refuse a click with Control and text with a line break, which can submit a form. `EngineKey` describes a key of the US layout as macOS does, for both adapters' events. Page scripts (`EnginePageScript`) run in each new document of a web contents, from its configuration's list or, after `setPageScripts`, from the next document on, at document start or once the document is parsed, in the main frame or every frame. Each is a function expression that the engine calls with its world's channel: `channel.send(value)` passes the value as JSON to the `pageScriptDelegate` and returns a promise of the reply, and `channel.onMessage(listener)` receives what `postPageScriptMessage(_:toWorld:)` delivers to the main frame. `executeJavaScript(_:inWorld:completion:)` calls a function with a world's channel in the main frame, opening the channel if the document has none, and returns the string it returns. Both adapters hand the delegate an `EnginePageScriptReply`, which sends the first reply only and sends nil for a reply the delegate drops, so a script's promise always settles. A page that a web contents opens starts with its scripts. Engines that run Manifest V3 web extensions name their extension runtime's version (`Engine.extensionRuntimeVersion`) and give each browser context an `EngineExtensions` (Tier 2): the app checks a package without starting it (`check(_:)`), loads an extension's package with the API permissions and sites the user granted (`EngineExtension`), unloads it, deletes its storage, reads and runs its action for a tab, allows it on a tab's site where the engine kept it from running (`grantSiteAccess(of:for:)`, for an action whose `wantsSiteAccess` is set), and reports its windows with their tabs and the focused one (`setWindows(_:focusedWindowID:)`). The engine asks its `EngineExtensionsDelegate` to open a tab or a window, which completes once the app has reported it, activate and close tabs, show an action's popup (`EngineExtensionPopup`: a popover that the engine filled, as WebKit's, or a popup page's view that the app shows in a popover of its own), ask about access an extension requests while it runs (`EngineExtensionAccess`) and the engine's own questions (`EngineExtensionPrompt`: more access in the engine's sentences or a reload of a tab), and pass native messages and ports as JSON. It also tells the delegate when an action changes.

### WebKit adapter

`ARCHEngineWebKit` adds to its user agent the version tokens that full browsers on this system send; without them, some sites serve pages for old browsers. A script in its own content world reports whether each document's media elements play sound. It describes the last element to play sound, or another element that still plays sound once that one stops, its position, and the page's Media Session metadata. A script in the page's world records the page's Media Session action handlers and runs them for the media keys. It reports and restores `interactionState` as a tab's restorable state, preserving the back-forward list and page positions. It enables element fullscreen, so the Fullscreen API moves the element into WebKit's own full-screen window, as in the system's browser.

It implements loading, reloading, stopping, back and forward navigation with the back-forward list, find with match counts (WebKit reports only whether a match exists; a script in its own content world counts matches in visible text, with a line break between the text of different blocks, as in the text WebKit's find searches, and places the selected one), viewport snapshots, and full-page captures from `createPDF` drawn off the main thread at the window's backing scale (or lower to keep every side within 16,384 pixels), because `takeSnapshot` draws only what is on screen, PDF export, which printing also uses (a paginated PDF on the system's default paper with the page's print styles, which WebKit's print operation saves without a panel, running modal for the window because `run()` never finishes for a web view; `createPDF` draws the whole page as one tall page and serves only a page outside a window), downloads with `WKDownload` (pause, resume, and cancel, which `WebKitDownloadLifecycle` decides apart from WebKit's objects: a pause keeps WebKit's resume data, and one without it cancels the download unless a resume waits; a resume while WebKit still stops the paused download waits for that data and continues from it, or, when WebKit leaves none, restarts the download from the start, which chooses the destination again (the save panel opens when Settings asks where to save); a second resume before WebKit hands over the restarted download does nothing; a failed download without resume data restarts from the start; and a finish completes the download whatever was asked of it meanwhile), popups, page-initiated closing, favicons, cookies, and whether the page uses the camera or the microphone (`cameraCaptureState` and `microphoneCaptureState`, active or muted). Every web view sits in a page view of its own and sets `isInspectable`, so the browser bundled with macOS can inspect it. While the private API flags allow it, ⌥⌘I opens WebKit's Web Inspector through the private `_inspector` object (`show`, then `attach`). WebKit attaches it beside the web view inside the page view and resizes the web view. The inspector stays with the tab across tab switches. Its toolbar docks it to another side or into its own window. Web views turn on developer extras (`_developerExtrasEnabled`), without which WebKit does not open the inspector, when they are made while the flags allow it and again before the inspector opens, so a tab made while the flags were off opens it too (needs verification, regression check 12). The page context menu still leaves WebKit's Inspect Element item out, as it holds only items both engines run (Decisions).

Its `WebKitWebView` subclass hands WebKit's context menu to the delegate in `willOpenMenu(_:with:)`, keeping only WebKit's own editing and spelling items (matched by tag), and learns the clicked link, image, and selection from a script in its own content world; menus a page element provides, such as a video's playback speed menu, and elements the script cannot see, such as those in a closed shadow root, keep WebKit's menu. AppKit adds its own items (AutoFill, Services, and Writing Tools where macOS offers it) to the menu, because WebKit shows it as a context menu for the web view; turning off `allowsContextMenuPlugIns` in `willOpenMenu(_:with:)` never removed them, because AppKit has started tracking the menu by then, so the adapter no longer sets it. It forwards JavaScript dialogs, with the asking frame's origin from `WKFrameInfo.securityOrigin`, HTTP authentication, links with schemes it does not load, media capture permission with the origin's port when it is not the default (after the system's own camera and microphone permission), and web content process termination to the delegate.

Each Persona's context has its own data store: the default Persona uses `WKWebsiteDataStore.default()`, and any other `WKWebsiteDataStore(forIdentifier:)` with the Persona's UUID, whose data, caches included, WebKit keeps in `~/Library/WebKit/xyz.taeyoon.arch/WebsiteDataStore/<uuid in lowercase>`. `removeStoredData` deletes such a store with `removeDataStoreForIdentifier:completionHandler:` at launch, before the engine starts. It deletes cookies accepted by a test through `WKHTTPCookieStore`. It removes other site data with `WKWebsiteDataStore.removeData`, using records that match the given origins' hosts, one per registrable domain. A closed page leaves its media presentations, pauses its media, and moves to a blank page without its delegates and observations, because a web view keeps its page as long as anything holds it. Neither its tab, whose closed-tab record keeps the page's address, nor the profile's extensions hear of the blank page.

Its navigation policy asks the navigation delegate about main frame navigations, again before each server redirect's request with the redirect's address, navigations without a target frame, and links clicked with Shift, and not about frames inside the page. It reports a link clicked with Shift and without Command, unless it is marked for download, as `newWindow`, as Chromium reads the click. It reads a page's readable text with that script through `callAsyncJavaScript` in its own content world. Page scripts run as `WKUserScript`s in a content world of their own per world, after a channel script that defines the world's channel there; a `WKScriptMessageHandlerWithReply` added only to that world carries the channel, and a world keeps its handler once added. The web contents' `WKUserContentController` subclass holds the page scripts. WebKit removes user scripts only all at once, so replacing page scripts adds the controller's other scripts again, telling them apart by a marker comment at the start of each page script's source. A popup uses its opener's content controller and so shares its page scripts. `executeJavaScript(_:inWorld:completion:)` runs the channel script first and then the function through `evaluateJavaScript(_:in:in:)`; `postPageScriptMessage` delivers through `callAsyncJavaScript`.

It answers `runOpenPanelWith` through the UI delegate: `allowsDirectories` asks for a folder and `allowsMultipleSelection` for several files, and the page's accepted types come from `WKOpenPanelParameters`' private `_acceptedMIMETypes` and `_acceptedFileExtensions` behind the private API flags (Private API flags); with a flag off, the request names no types. Without a UI delegate the page gets no file, and a delegate that lets the completion go gives the page a cancel, because WebKit raises an exception when it releases an open panel's completion handler that was never called. The request names the frame that asks (`initiatedByFrame`'s security origin) and the page: the main frame's own origin, or for a frame inside the page that of the address the main frame last committed, which the adapter records when WebKit reports the commit (`webView(_:didCommit:)`), because the web view's address and its current back-forward item can already be those of a navigation the page started and has not committed: the item moves at the start of a back or forward navigation, also one that `history.back()` starts (`WebKitFileChooserTests`). `evaluateJavaScript` runs with a user gesture, so a script's `click()` on a file input opens the chooser, which `WebKitFileChooserTests` uses; through `executeJavaScript(_:inWorld:completion:)`, which runs without one while the private API flags allow it, the same `click()` opens none. The gesture reaches the page's code that the script calls, such as a DOM method the page replaced, and the timers the script schedules, but leaves the page no activation once it returns (`WebKitFileChooserTests`). So the adapter's own scripts run in worlds of the app's own, where the page's replacements do not apply: the favicon lookup after every load runs in `.defaultClient`, as find does.

Its trusted input (`WebKitInput`) sends `NSEvent`s made with public constructors to the web view's responder methods: clicks to `mouseDown` and `mouseUp` with no pointer move first, which reach the page in any window, pointer moves only in the key window, where WebKit fires `mousemove`, a wheel turn made from a `CGEvent` with its deltas clamped to ±100,000 points (`EngineWheelTurn`), and named keys other than Space only while the web view is the first responder of the key window, or of its window while no window is key, never with Command or Control and with no function key flag, so AppKit offers none to the menus. WebKit hands a key down that the page leaves unhandled back with `NSApplication.sendEvent(_:)` once the page has answered, which can be after another window or view became key, so the app's `NSApplication` subclass (`ArchApplication`) drops every key down the input sent there, before AppKit's local event monitors, which it calls in no fixed order, the menus, and the key window (`WebKitEngine.isTrustedInputKey(_:)`, over a weak table of the sent keys); `WebKitWebView` drops one that reaches it through its window. Character keys and Space are refused, because the user's input source could type another character, such as a full-width space; text goes through `insertText(_:)`, also into a page that no window shows.

From macOS 15.4, the engine has the `extensions` capability, and each profile has one `WKWebExtensionController` (`WebKitExtensions`), which every web view of the profile, popups included, uses. Its configuration follows the profile's store: non-persistent for a store that keeps nothing on disk, which only the adapter's tests make, the default configuration for the default store, and otherwise `WKWebExtensionController.Configuration(identifier:)` with the store's identifier; extensions reach cookies in the profile's store. An extension loads as a `WKWebExtensionContext` whose `uniqueIdentifier` is the app's identifier for it and whose base URL is `webkit-extension://<identifier>/`, so its storage lasts across launches, with the granted API permissions and match patterns, inspectable from the browser bundled with macOS. Each web contents keeps a `WKWebExtensionTab` object for its lifetime, which answers from its web view and from the windows the app last reported and passes activating and closing to the delegate; its title, address, and loading changes reach the extensions. `setWindows` keeps a `WKWebExtensionWindow` object for each window while the window stays, lists only the profile's own web contents, and tells the controller, in this order, which windows opened, which tabs closed, opened, and moved to another window, which windows closed with the tabs that closed with them, each window's active tab, and the focused window, so a tab leaves a closing window before the window closes; a tab that only changed its place in a window is not reported. The controller delegate lists the windows with the focused one first, hands an action's popup over as WebKit's own `popupPopover`, which sizes itself to the popup and closes when the popup closes itself, for the app to show, and closes the popup when the app cannot, opens the tabs that extensions ask for, options pages among them, and windows with their addresses through the app, following only a window's addresses and focus, turns permission, address, and match pattern prompts into `EngineExtensionAccess` questions whose answers last while the extension stays loaded, and passes native messages and ports as JSON. WebKit loads an extension's page only in a web view made from the configuration the extension's context gives (`webViewConfiguration`), and loads no other page there. So a web contents that starts at a loaded extension's page, as a tab that `tabs.create` opens at `runtime.getURL(…)` or an options page does, starts from that configuration, and a web page that such a page opens in a new window gets a web contents of its own, without an opener. A web contents keeps its web view, so its navigation policy cancels a main frame navigation to a loaded extension's page from a web view of another kind, or to a web page from an extension's web view, whether a link, a script, `tabs.update`, Back, or the address field starts it, and on the next turn of the run loop hands its UI delegate a replacement from the profile with its page scripts, which loads the page with a back-forward list of its own; of several such navigations in one turn, the last loads. Any navigation out to a web page gets a replacement, but one into an extension's page gets one only when the app starts it through the navigation controller, as the address field and `tabs.update` do, which the web contents notes by the address it loads, when a frame of the same extension starts it, or when the extension's `web_accessible_resources` names the page for the starting frame's origin or extension; a web page's own link, script, redirect, or new window to any other page of an extension loads nothing (Decisions). The replacement's extension tab names the old one, so `setWindows` reports `didReplaceTab(_:with:)` once the app lists the replacement in the old tab's place, with no close, open, or activation. A web contents that opens at a page of an extension the profile has not loaded, as a restored tab does before the app loads the extensions, loads nothing and reports the page's address and saved state until the extension loads, when a replacement restores that state, or until another navigation goes ahead in it (Decisions). The navigation policy hands the `webkit-extension` scheme to WebKit rather than to other apps, so a page also loads an extension's web-accessible pages in its frames, as a password manager's menu. Content scripts run only in pages of a persistent store, because WebKit treats web views of a non-persistent store as private. `removeStoredData` also deletes the extension data records of the storage identifier; WebKit keeps a small state file for each extension it loaded there, which no public API removes.

### Chromium adapter

`ARCHEngineChromium` embeds a CEF browser (Alloy style) beside the sidebar through `CEFBridge`, with an external message pump on the main run loop, whose timer runs in the run loop's common modes so that menus and app-modal alerts do not hold CEF's work, and the `ArchHelper` subprocess. CEF writes no log (`log_severity` is off for the browser and its helpers), since Chromium's `chrome_debug.log` under the root cache path kept pages' console messages with their script addresses and downloads' names outside every Persona; the file an earlier build left goes at start. It implements loading, navigation with the back-forward list (from CEF's navigation entries; selecting an entry checks its URL against the menu's URL, stops any main frame navigation in progress, then calls the page's `history.go`, because CEF has no equivalent call and the DevTools protocol's call would leave a debugger attached), title, URL, and loading state, favicons from Chromium's icon URLs (Chromium reports them once a document finishes loading and never for a page back from the back-forward cache, so the icon stays while the tab stays on one host, also through same-document navigations such as `history.pushState`, until the next report's icon replaces it, which an earlier report's icon that loads later never does, and a page on another host starts with the icon that host's pages last showed in the tab, or with none), zoom level, popups as new tabs that keep `window.opener`, closing, restored tabs that load their last address (CEF cannot fill a back-forward list), whether the page uses the camera or the microphone (CEF's `OnMediaAccessChange`, which the bridge passes on), the page's audible state and media (the helper's renderer process adds a script to each frame before page scripts run; the script reports media elements and Media Session metadata, describing the same element as WebKit's script, records the page's action handlers, and runs them or plays and pauses the media through `ExecuteJavaScript` for the media keys), fullscreen requests from the Fullscreen API (CEF sizes the content; the app transitions the window and ends fullscreen with `ExitFullscreen`), the accessibility tree of every page while VoiceOver runs or while an assistive app asks for it by setting `AXEnhancedUserInterface` or `AXManualAccessibility` on the application (a private API behind flags), find with match counts, PDF export, which printing also uses, on the system's default paper (CEF's default is US Letter), viewport and full-page capture through the DevTools protocol's `Page.captureScreenshot` (`captureBeyondViewport` for the whole page), as images sized in points, a capture still waiting when the page's process quits or the browser closes ending without an image (the first capture attaches a DevTools client that stays until the browser closes, so a captured page that hangs in its `beforeunload` handler keeps its tab open; Decisions), downloads (pause, resume, cancel, refusal; Chromium asks for the destination again each time it resumes an interrupted download, on its own or through Resume, and such a download keeps its item and the destination it has, which holds its partial file), media and permission prompts routed to the delegate (camera and microphone also ask the system, and a request for a device the system denies the app is denied without asking; Chromium stores its permission prompt's answers in the profile and reuses them without asking, so `setRememberedPermission` sets those content settings through `CefRequestContext::SetContentSetting` to match the app's remembered answers), and renderer crash recovery (a reload after each crash, and a blank page when the page crashes again within a minute of its last reload, `repeatedCrashInterval`).

Closing a browser detaches its view in `DoClose`, so CEF never closes the app's window, also when the app has already released the browser object. CEF closes the browser only once that view is released. Before the view leaves, a page that has the window's focus gives it up, and once it has left, the windows update at once, as Chromium's own page view does when it goes: AppKit can hold the focused view, as through the text input context, until it next updates its windows, which it does after each event, and a page that closes itself brings none. The page is also muted, so a closed page is not heard while anything still holds its view. CEF creates and closes browsers on later tasks, so the bridge keeps each browser object, with its container view and CEF's view, from `CreateBrowser` until `OnAfterCreated` and from the app's close until `OnBeforeClose`, as it keeps a popup until CEF creates it; a close that comes before `OnAfterCreated` takes effect then, as does the latest load the app asked for before then, such as an address the user entered while a tab's page started, and releasing an open browser that the app did not close closes it. A page that closes itself has no such entry, so the bridge keeps the object itself until it has handled `OnBeforeClose`, whose delegate call can release the last reference. JavaScript `alert`, `confirm`, `prompt`, and `beforeunload` dialogs go to the delegate through `CefJSDialogHandler`, the first three with the asking frame's origin from `OnJSDialog`'s `origin_url`, and closing a tab runs the page's `beforeunload` handlers first, except while the tab has a download in progress or interrupted: asking would mean closing the browser, which stops the download (CEF stops reporting a closed browser's downloads but lets them run on, so the bridge cancels the ones in progress or interrupted as the browser closes, which also removes an interrupted one's partial file), so that tab closes at once, and its kept page answers its own handlers with Leave when it moves to a blank page. While the runtime runs, a page's own `window.close()`, or its opener's, does not close a browser that such a download needs: `DoClose` leaves the browser's view in place, so CEF keeps the browser open, and the delegate learns on the next turn of the run loop that the page closed itself. The bridge tells these closes from the app's own, which `close` and `tryCloseWithCompletion:` request. `CefContextMenuHandler::RunContextMenu` hands each context click to the delegate: the bridge copies CEF's parameters, shows the app's `NSMenu` from a run loop block once CEF's current work returns, so CEF keeps running while the menu tracks, and cancels CEF's request exactly once when the menu closes or the browser goes, so Chromium's own menu and commands never run. Its spelling and edit items run on the frame the menu opened on. The adapter shows the menu through `EngineContextMenuPresenter` (`ARCHEngine`) with `NSMenu.popUpContextMenu(_:with:for:)`, so AppKit adds its own items where it adds them to WebKit's menu. The menu opens at the click's own mouse event while that is still the app's current event and lies at the click, as Chromium's own context menu code uses it (`ui::EventForPositioningContextMenuRelativeToWindow`), or else at a right mouse down the presenter makes at the click with the current time. In a field, the view it names is the one that adopts `NSTextInputClient` under the click, Chromium's `RenderWidgetHostViewCocoa`, found by hit testing CEF's view, while that view has a text input context, which Chromium withholds in a password field and in one that has been one except while the bridge's `setContextMenuPasswordAutoFill` call is on; the presenter turns it on for a field's menu, right before it chooses the view, and off once the menu closes (VI.9). AppKit adds AutoFill for the context and Services for the field's selection; in a password field, Chromium's view offers its text to no service while the call is on. On a link outside a field, also when the right click selected a text link's text, as Blink does on macOS, the presenter puts a small requestor view at the click for the menu's time, which hands Services the link's address as a URL and as text, as WebKit does, under both the current type names and the older ones AppKit asks by. Elsewhere, and in a field without a text input context, it names CEF's container view, which offers AppKit nothing, so that no service gets a selection outside a field, which WebKit's view does not offer, or reads or replaces a password.

Cookies, stored data, and permission settings go through each Persona's request context (`ARCHCEFRequestContext`): the default Persona uses CEF's global context, whose profile is `Default` under the root cache path `Cache/Chromium`, and any other Persona a context whose profile is `Persona-<UUID>` beside it. Browsers use a new context at once; its cookie, storage, and permission calls wait in order until Chromium reports the context and its cookie store initialized. Browsers of different Personas never share a render process, and a popup keeps its opener's context. Chromium keeps a profile's HTTP and code caches at the same relative path under `~/Library/Caches`. `removeStoredData` removes nothing while CEF runs; at launch it renames a Persona's profile directories under both roots and deletes them in the background, and `makeApplication` deletes renamed leftovers before CEF starts.

HTTP authentication requests for sites and proxies reach the delegate through `GetAuthCredentials` as a `URLAuthenticationChallenge`, with a failure count the adapter keeps until a page finishes loading; the `disable-chrome-login-prompt` switch keeps Chromium's own sign-in window from taking them first. Navigations and popups to schemes Chromium does not load stop in `OnBeforeBrowse` and `OnBeforePopup` and go to the delegate as links for other apps.

A tab's or preview's file chooser, a page's file input or another such as a download's Save As, reaches the bridge's `CefDialogHandler::OnFileDialog` of its browser, which returns true, so Chromium's own panel does not open, also for a browser without an owner, which gets a cancel. CEF's file dialog factory takes every chooser and asks the dialog handler of the browser it finds for the chooser's web contents, then for its window, then the focused browser, and cancels when it finds none (`file_dialog_runner.cc`, read in CEF's source). An extension's popup and docked DevTools have no browser of their own, so their choosers come to another browser's `OnFileDialog`, a tab of the same window or the focused one, under that tab's site, or get a cancel; which one happens is not checked (regression check 85). The bridge passes CEF's mode, the page's accepted types (`accept_filters`, not CEF's expansion into extensions), and the main frame's committed address (`CefFrame::GetURL()`, which CEF reads from `GetLastCommittedURL()`), because `OnFileDialog` names no frame; the request names that page and no frame. The adapter suggests only the file name of CEF's default path for saving; Arch's downloads never ask CEF for Save As, since they always give a destination. CEF cancels a chooser whose callback is released unanswered and ignores a second answer.

Its developer tools open through `ShowDevTools` with a layer-backed container view of the bridge's as the parent view. Arch's engine fills the container with the DevTools front-end, in Chromium's docked mode without a DevTools browser and without the front-end's docking controls (an experimental CEF API, VI.3). The bridge reports the container when `HasDevTools()` is true right after the call, which CEF runs at once on the main thread. It reports the close at `CefLifeSpanHandler::OnDevToolsInViewClosed`, or at `OnBeforeClose` when the browser closes first and CEF does not report the tools' close. CEF holds only a raw handle to the container, so the browser's client keeps it until then, even after the browser object is gone. While the tools close, from when `HasDevTools()` turns false until that report, `showDevTools` does nothing. A discarded web contents reports its tools closed at once, because CEF's report may come after the web contents has gone. The tools pass the keys they do not handle to the inspected browser, so they reach the main menu as the page's keys do. At quit, the runtime first closes the tools in views and runs CEF's work until they report their close, then closes every browser and runs CEF's work until they close, for up to two seconds in all. In that second wait, browsers that no view shows, those of Chromium's own windows and the hidden ones that clear site data, count as open until they close. It then shuts down. The tools go first because their front-end's contents would otherwise close on their own after their browser, unreported, while CEF shut down. Shutting down with browsers open crashed after Chrome-style developer tools had opened. A quit that starts inside CEF's work, as ⌘Q does when a page or an extension popup leaves the key to the main menu, runs again from a timer once that work has returned, because CEF runs none of the closes from inside it.

It deletes cookies accepted by a test by visiting the context's cookie manager and reports after that store flushes. It clears an origin's other storage with the DevTools protocol's `Storage.clearDataForOrigin` in a separate hidden browser of the same context. That browser then closes, so no tab keeps a DevTools client, and a quit meanwhile waits for its answers and its close within the two seconds. The bridge turns off Chromium's own media key handling and Now Playing (`--disable-features=HardwareMediaKeyHandling`), which would use the same process-wide `MPNowPlayingInfoCenter` and `MPRemoteCommandCenter` as the app.

The bridge's `OnBeforeBrowse` asks the navigation delegate about every main frame navigation, after its check for links to other apps, and CEF calls it again with `is_redirect` before a server redirect's request; regression check 75 confirms that a refused hop sends nothing. `OnOpenURLFromTab` and `OnBeforePopup` ask the navigation delegate about a link the user clicked with Shift, which Chromium gives the new window disposition; a link the delegate allows loads in place, as Alloy style does, or opens as a popup. It reads a page's readable text through a process message to the helper's renderer process, which evaluates the script in the main frame's page world and replies with its JSON string (`evaluateScript:completion:`), because `ExecuteJavaScript` returns no value and the DevTools protocol would attach a debugger; a request without a reply ends after ten seconds, when the renderer quits, or when the browser closes. Page scripts run in the page's main world: the bridge passes them to the renderer in the browser's extra info, and in a process message to each of the browser's frames when they change and to each frame created after a change; each renderer keeps them per browser object. CEF does not order that message before the frame's first document, so in a renderer process that had none of the browser's frames, such as after a cross-site navigation, the first document may still run the scripts the browser was created with (needs verification). The helper's `OnContextCreated` makes the document's channel opener before the page's scripts run, so every channel, also one that `executeJavaScript(_:inWorld:completion:)` opens later, calls the built-ins as they were then. It opens each world's channel with a native function that it passes to the channel script as an argument, so it never reaches `window`, and runs the scripts before the page's own; channels keep their state in objects without prototypes, and document-end scripts wait for `DOMContentLoaded`. The page can still see the values a script sends and receives through built-ins it replaces, such as a `then` getter on `Object.prototype` for object replies. Values a script sends carry a request number unique in its renderer process; the browser answers through the sending frame. `executeJavaScript(_:inWorld:completion:)` shares the process message of `readableContent`, and `postPageScriptMessage` sends one to the main frame.

Its trusted input (`ChromiumInput`) goes through the bridge's methods over `CefBrowserHost::SendMouseMoveEvent`, `SendMouseClickEvent`, `SendMouseWheelEvent`, and `SendKeyEvent` while the browser's view is in a window. A click sends no pointer move before its presses, and wheel deltas are clamped to ±100,000 points (`EngineWheelTurn`), within CEF's `int`. Text goes as one character event per character in the Basic Multilingual Plane, because `ImeCommitText` serves only windowless browsers. CEF builds the AppKit event of a key sent this way with window number 0, and the bridge's `CefKeyboardHandler::OnKeyEvent` stops such a key when the page leaves it unhandled, before CEF offers it to the main menu; the user's keys carry their window's number and pass. Whether the page receives each event as trusted is checked from the bundle (check 84).

For a popup it does not stop, `OnBeforePopup` hands CEF a browser object of the bridge's own, with its own client, a container view of the opener's size, and the opener's page scripts in its extra info, and returns false, so CEF creates the popup in the opener's browsing context and request context, as CEF's sample client does on macOS. The page keeps the window `window.open` returns, and the popup keeps `window.opener`, which sign-in popups need to report back. `OnBeforePopup` stops a popup while the runtime shuts down, when the opener's `ChromiumWebContents` has no UI delegate to adopt it, as WebKit stops one, and for a Document Picture-in-Picture window, which would become a tab, so the page keeps its own interface. The bridge holds the object until the popup's `OnAfterCreated`, when the opener's `ChromiumWebContents` wraps it in a new one and reports it to the UI delegate with Chromium's disposition, or until `OnBeforePopupAborted` drops it. CEF owns a popup's page only once the opener shows the popup, which Chromium does after `OnAfterCreated` in the same task, and closing the popup before then crashes CEF, as CEF's `CreateInternal` and `AddNewContents` read the browser's released platform delegate. So a popup that the UI delegate dropped closes on the next turn of the run loop.

A video a page puts in picture in picture plays through AVKit in the system's window (VI.2). The bridge's client gives Arch's engine a picture-in-picture handler, so Chromium's own window never opens; the engine captures the video's frames into IOSurfaces and reports its start, with the video's place in the page, its playback state, and its end, all on the main thread. `ChromiumPictureInPicture` puts an `AVSampleBufferDisplayLayer` in a view below the CEF browser view, at the video's place in the container view, where the page covers it, and shows it through an `AVPictureInPictureController` as live content: play and pause only, no timeline, and no skip buttons. Each frame's IOSurface becomes a sample buffer without a copy, shown at once, with a clean aperture for the frame's content rectangle; the engine leaves a surface unchanged until three later frames have arrived. The window's play and pause button reaches the page's Media Session handlers or the video through `SetPictureInPicturePlaying`, and does nothing for a video the page offers no control for, such as a camera stream. Its close button ends picture in picture through `ClosePictureInPicture`, pausing the video unless the page offers no control, and its return button asks the UI delegate to show the tab (`webContentsDidRequestActivation`), then ends picture in picture without pausing. AVKit asks to restore the interface for every stop, also one the app requests, so that request counts as the return button only while the engine has not ended picture in picture. When the page ends picture in picture, navigates, crashes, or closes, or a video in another tab enters picture in picture, the adapter stops AVKit's session; a second video in the same tab replaces the first in the same session. Stops stalled in the prototype (`picture-in-picture.md` in `arch-chromium`, fact 29 and Risk 12): the controller's stop led to no delegate call while the layer's view was in no window, as a page's view is while another tab is selected, and releasing the controller's content source ended a stop only while the view was in a window. So before a stop that the engine asks for, the adapter moves a source view that is in no window to the top left of the content view of the app's front visible window that can become key, below the window's content, and a stop that has not finished a second after it began ends when the adapter releases the content source. This does not help while the app is hidden, where the prototype's stops stalled too. The prototype ran with the screen locked, and its stop checks have not run again with the screen unlocked, as the design's Order of work asks before the app commit: whether stops stall on a drawn screen, and whether the moved source view lets them end, is unverified (regression check 51). A new session immediately ends one still stopping, and a closed web contents stops its session, which keeps itself until it finishes. None of this has run in the app yet (regression checks 50 and 51).

Web extensions run in each profile's own Chromium extension system through the extension runtime that Arch's engine adds to CEF (an experimental API, VI.1), while every tab stays an Alloy style browser. `ChromiumExtensions`, which each context makes as it is created, loads an installed package unpacked from its folder, whose manifest's `key` gives it Arch's identifier, switches it on, and sets its host permissions to every site its manifest asks for when the grant has them all, comparing schemes and hosts in lowercase and leaving out the patterns for `file:` and `ftp:` pages, which no extension reaches on Arch's engine. Otherwise, as also for a pattern with a port or one for another scheme, such as `wss:`, which no grant holds, it passes the grant's sites with the click setting (VI.5): Chromium grants the manifest's sites that they cover as runtime host permissions, which the profile keeps across reloads and launches, so the extension runs there without a click, and withholds the others until a click or Allow on This Site. A `wss:` pattern is never covered, and a pattern with a port only where a granted pattern covers its host, since Arch's patterns and Chromium's cover every port of their host. Each load replaces the sites that the previous load granted, and a load with the same grant changes nothing. Allow on This Site grants the origin of the tab's page as one more runtime host permission, next to the grant's sites, and runs what Chromium kept from running there or asks to reload the tab; the sites it allowed, and those from `permissions.request()`, stay through later loads unless they cover every site or the grant moves from every site to some. Chromium grants the API permissions the manifest asks for, which are those the install grants. Chromium keeps the extensions it loaded in the profile and starts them with it, so once the app says so (`stopUnloadedExtensions()`), after the runtime's first pass of loads, as the profile starts while the Persona has no enabled extension, or before Arch's links let more extensions reach a native app, the adapter switches off every extension that the app has not loaded or is not loading in the run as soon as Chromium lists it, and the first load in a run of a package installed before the engine started (`EngineExtension.installedAt`) that Chromium already has from the same folder and version keeps Chromium's copy instead of installing it again; later loads, and packages installed in the run, reload the package. Unloading switches an extension off, and deleting its storage uninstalls it. Chromium applies a switch-off only once the profile's extensions have loaded, after it has started those it kept, so `waitForUnloads()` reads Chromium's list every 20 milliseconds until it shows none of what the app stopped as running, for up to five seconds after Chromium's first answer. Arch's engine loads the runtime's packages without file access, also when Chromium reloads them or keeps them from an earlier launch, so an extension runs on no `file:` page in Chromium tabs, even with host permissions that apply on every site, and `tabs.create` and `tabs.update` refuse `file:` addresses. CEF's runtime has no call that reads a package without installing it, so `check(_:)` reads the files for what keeps Chromium's loader from running a Manifest V3 package: a manifest that does not read, a background of `background.scripts` or `background.page` alone, which Chromium leaves out of Manifest V3, and a service worker, content script, icon, or default locale file that the package lacks; anything else shows when the package loads. The extension runtime's version is the CEF version. Each reported window becomes a client window (`CefExtensionWindow`) that lists the profile's tabs in the window's order with its active tab and focus; CEF reads a client window's frame and state from an AppKit window it keeps for the window's life, while the AppKit window that shows a space changes and can be none, so each client window sits over a hidden stand-in window of the adapter's, which answers with the frame and the minimized, zoomed, and full screen state of the window that shows it at each question, never shows, and does nothing for `windows.remove` and focus requests. The bridge lists a browser once CEF has created it, answers a request for a tab once a window lists the new tab, and answers a request for a window once it lists every tab the app gave it, or with none when one of them closes first. Chromium's own windows, as `identity.launchWebAuthFlow` opens, get the bridge's default client, which logs and closes each one once CEF has created it, and shutdown waits for them. The popover reads the actions from CEF with their icons at the window's scale, including the disabled action that Chromium makes for an extension without one; a popup page's view reaches the app, which sizes it as the page asks, and the keys the page leaves reach the main menu; requests for tabs, windows, and prompts reach the delegate, a store install's prompt among them as `install`, with the extension's identifier and the store page's tab. Chromium's own native messaging reads host manifests from `NativeMessagingHosts` under the root cache path, which the engine links to the app's folder of that name before CEF starts while nothing is at that path (whether Chromium follows the link needs verification). None of this has run in the app yet (regression check 52).

Chromium keeps its cookie encryption key in the login keychain (item `Chromium Safe Storage`), which every build signed with the same identity reads without a prompt. Cookies that earlier builds saved under Chromium's mock keychain cannot be decrypted with that key, so Chromium sign-ins started over once.

### Private API flags

`ARCHPrivateAPI` keeps the private APIs registered by engines and features. Each API has an availability check that runs on a background queue when the API is registered, or at first use if it has not finished; a check at first use is recorded after that turn of the main actor, since the flags page asks while it draws. The result lasts until the app quits. The WebKit engine registers its APIs when it is created, and its application the evaluation without a user gesture when it is made, so the flags page lists them before WebKit starts. Flags for all private APIs, each API, and each feature are on by default and saved in `UserDefaults`. A feature may use its APIs only while all its flags are on and every API exists. The Chromium engine registers the accessibility attributes that assistive apps set on the application when it starts.

### Browsing module

#### Window and sidebar

Each browser window shows the system's translucent sidebar material with a wash of its current space's tint, and a sidebar with a top row on the line of the window's traffic light buttons (the sidebar button, then back, forward, and reload, which becomes stop while a page loads; in full screen the sidebar button takes the traffic lights' place), the address pill, favorites, one page per space with its pinned tabs, its open tabs, and a New Tab row below them, and a bottom bar with the library button at its leading end and, centered on the sidebar independently of that button (shifted 2 points right for visual alignment), one symbol per space followed by a small, dim plus for a new space, 20 points apart; when they do not fit beside the library button, the symbols and the plus scroll sideways together. Three items appear at full size: the current space and its neighbors, or the three at the end nearest it. The others shrink with their distance from those three. While scrolling, items also start shrinking three items before either end of the strip so none is cut off. The strip centers the current space when it changes. Dragging the sidebar's trailing edge sets its width between 240 and 400 points; the width persists across launches. Hiding the sidebar slides it and the window buttons away and lets the page fill the window. The card appears only when the pointer next reaches the window's leading edge. The sidebar then slides in over the page as a card with 16-point corners; on macOS 15 and later, the window buttons slide in with it. The card hides when the pointer leaves, unless a menu, popover, sheet, or command palette opened from it remains open or the user is dragging one of its tabs, folders, favorites, or space symbols. The card's sidebar button docks the sidebar in the card's position. The card stays above the page while the page narrows, then gives way to the docked sidebar. With no tab open, the controls stay, the address pill shows a placeholder and opens a new tab, and the page frame shows the window's material and the space's tint, slightly darker than the sidebar. While macOS's Low Power Mode is on, the address pill shows a battery symbol with a yellow level and an outline in the bar's secondary color before its share button, sized like its other buttons, and a small window's top row shows the same before its share button (`LowPowerModeIndicator`); its help says pages may animate more slowly, and a click or its context menu's Low Power Mode Settings… opens the mode's System Settings pane, Battery or, on a Mac without a battery, Energy. `LowPowerMode` reads `ProcessInfo.isLowPowerModeEnabled` at launch and again at each `NSProcessInfoPowerStateDidChangeNotification`, which it takes on the main queue. Arch never changes the mode and offers no way to ignore it for itself alone (see Decisions).

#### Dragging and dropping

Dragging a tab lifts it above the window so it follows the pointer anywhere, including outside the list. While the pointer is over the rows, they move aside, leaving a dimmed empty slot and giving a light haptic at each new position. On release, the tab returns to its slot wherever the pointer is. Its shadow and the slot's dimming fade in and out over 0.4 seconds. Dropping it among pinned or open tabs pins or unpins it. Releasing a tab over another space's symbol in the bottom bar, which lights up with a light haptic while the tab is over it, moves the tab after that space's own pinned or open tabs and shrinks the lifted row into the symbol; a selected tab takes the sidebar to that space. Folder rows drag as tab rows do (Folders). Favorites and space symbols drag the same way: the item lifts above the window, the others make room with a light haptic, and the order persists in `session.json`; only the symbol strip animates a space's move, so the current page stays in view. Links, files, and text that is an address, dropped from other apps or pages onto the favorites and tab list, open in new tabs of the current space in the default engine, in the order dropped, while a ring around the list shows where they land; other text and links with other schemes, such as `mailto:`, open nothing. A drag that reaches the window's leading edge while the sidebar is hidden shows the card.

#### Tab rows

Double-clicking a tab or choosing Rename Tab in its context menu renames it in place. An empty name or the page's own title restores the page's title; `session.json` stores the names. ⌘Z reopens the tabs the user just closed, newest first, until the user presses a different key or clicks in the page. The monitor compares each key with the current shortcuts of Edit ▸ Undo, Close Tab, Close Other Tabs, and Reopen Last Closed Tab, so it follows changes in Settings ▸ Shortcuts. Holding ⌘ alone for 0.2 seconds fades in the number from each tab's ⌘-number shortcut in place of its close button. Releasing ⌘ immediately fades the numbers out. Once a key or a click uses the held ⌘, as ⌘L or a ⌘-click does, the numbers stay hidden until ⌘ is released; ⌘1 to ⌘9 keep shown numbers. Under the pointer, the close button appears instead. A tab row shows a speaker after its title while its page plays sound. A tab that a page opens behind the selected tab shows a dot in place of its close button until selected. This applies to links from the context menu's Open Link in New Tab and popups from unselected pages, which open in their page's space without taking the selection. Hovering a tab row shows its full title and address.

#### Spaces

The sidebar has several spaces, each with a tint. Each space has a menu, from the button beside its name and from the context menu of its symbol, to rename it (also by double-clicking the name), choose its theme (Automatic or one of seven colors) and symbol, move it left or right, add a space, and delete it; a deleted space's tabs move to its neighbor. A two-finger horizontal swipe over the sidebar, above the bottom bar, drags the space pages with the fingers and settles on the nearest space with a spring and a haptic at the halfway point; the switcher and the Spaces menu slide the pages the same way. Each space page scrolls on its own. While rows are hidden past an end of the list, that end fades out over 20 points, under the address field or above the bottom bar, instead of cutting the rows off; an end with nothing past it stays sharp. A row that opens or closes fades, a closing one shrinking slightly, while the rows after it slide into place; moves during a drag keep their own timing. Each space can have a home page. Set Current Page as Home in the space's menu stores the selected tab's web address with the space in `session.json`. Clear Home removes it. History ▸ Home (⇧⌘H) loads the current space's home page in the selected tab, or in a new tab when none is selected. It is disabled without a home page and under an internal page. Each space's theme is one of seven colors or Automatic. Automatic follows the accent color chosen in System Settings, including changes while the app runs; `session.json` stores the theme as the space's `tint`. The window resolves the current space's tint in its own appearance and washes the window material with it at the strength chosen in Settings ▸ Appearance. When the sidebar's text on that wash falls below WCAG's 4.5:1 contrast and the other appearance's text has more contrast, the sidebar and a dragged tab row use the other appearance. A space symbol whose tint contrasts less than 1.5:1 with the wash takes the sidebar's text color.

#### Folders

Folders group a space's pinned or open tabs and nest up to four levels deep (bookmark rule 6; `docs/bookmarks.md`, Nested folders). Each list starts with its folders; each folder's row is followed by its own folders and then its tabs, each level indented 12 points further. `FolderTree` gives the rows, the order, and the moves, and the session and the views ask it instead of walking `TabFolder.parentID`. Clicking a folder's row collapses or expands it; a collapsed folder hides everything inside it, at every level, but the selected tab, which shows one level in from the folder. New Folder in the space's menu adds an empty folder at the top of the pinned list. A folder's context menu has Rename Folder, New Folder Inside (disabled at the fourth level and in live folders), Move to Folder, Move Out of Folder, and Delete Folder. A tab's has Add to New Folder, which makes the folder where the tab is and is disabled in a folder at the fourth level, Move to Folder, and Move Out of Folder. Move to Folder is a submenu that mirrors the tree, for a tab the pinned list's folders before the open list's, in which a folder that holds listed folders is a submenu whose first item moves into the folder itself. It offers only the moves `FolderTree` allows: a tab into an ordinary folder of its space other than its own, taking the folder's list, and a folder into another ordinary folder of its list that is not itself, a folder inside it, or its parent, where it and the folders it holds stay within the fourth level. Move Out of Folder moves a tab or a folder up one level: a tab first among the tabs there, a folder right after the folder it leaves. A new folder opens its name field, expanding the folders that hold it, and Rename Folder renames it in place; an empty name keeps the old one. A tab dragged over another folder's row, at any level, lights the row up with a light haptic and, on release, goes to the end of that folder, taking its pinned state, while the lifted row shrinks into the folder's row. Dragging a tab among a folder's tabs moves it into the folder, and among the tabs outside folders moves it out. Dragging a folder's row lifts it as a tab's, with the rows inside it hidden until release and their height kept below the list's end, so that a scrolled list does not shift under the pointer (`SidebarDragItem.folder`), and `FolderTree.dropTarget(for:at:)` decides where it goes. Over the upper third of another folder's row, the rows make room for it before that folder, at that folder's level; over a tab's row, last among the folders of the tab's folder; over a tab outside folders, the divider, or the New Tab row, last at the top of its list; each new place gives a light haptic. Over the rest of a folder's row, that row lights up with a light haptic and, on release, takes it last among its folders while the lifted row shrinks into it; its own parent's row does so while another folder follows it there. Released over another space's symbol of its Persona, it moves with everything it holds to the end of that space's folders in the same list, its tabs leave that space's split view, and a window that selects one of them follows it. A dragged folder stays in its list and never goes into itself or a folder inside it, past the fourth level with the folders it holds, or where a collapsed folder would hide it; where the tree refuses a place, nothing moves, and on release the row returns to its slot. The release does not collapse or expand the folder. Pinning or unpinning a tab, or dropping it on another space's symbol, takes it out of its folder. Delete Folder keeps what the folder holds: its folders take its place in its parent, or at the top of its list, and its tabs go first among the tabs there. A deleted space's folders move to its neighbor with its tabs and keep their tree. Nothing enters a live folder, and its tabs keep their source's order (Live folders below). `session.json` stores the folders with their parents (version 15), their collapsed state, live folders' configurations (version 16), and each tab's folder. Loading moves a folder to the top of its list when its parent is missing, in another space or list, or part of a cycle, or when the folder sits at the fifth level, taking the folders inside it along; a file before version 15 loads its folders at the top level. A reopened or restored tab returns to its folder while the folder is still in that list. Browser import adds its folders at the top of the pinned list, with nested names joined (`docs/import-browsers.md`). Regression check 70 covers the menus, rows, and drags in the app.

#### Split view

Split view shows two to four tabs of one space side by side in the page area, in panes of equal width 8 points apart. Tab ▸ Open in Split View (⌥⌘=) puts the selected tab in its space's split view or, without one, beside the tab selected before it. A tab row's Open in Split View opens that tab as a pane after the panes on screen. A tab dragged from the sidebar onto a pane opens before or after it, by the half under the pointer, which lights up with a light haptic. A split view started beside a tab shown alone replaces the space's earlier one. The page area shows a space's split view while one of its tabs is selected. The selected tab is the focused pane, marked by a ring in the space's tint, and a click in another pane selects that pane's tab. The address pill, the find bar, the question popover, the menus, the internal page, the region capture overlay, page fullscreen, and docked developer tools follow the focused pane; each pane shows its own tab's prompt card, and only the focused pane's card takes the keys. The other panes' rows show a lighter selection. Each pane's close control takes its tab out of the split view and leaves the tab open; on the focused pane it selects the pane beside it, or else another pane, that no other window shows. Closing the focused pane's tab selects the pane beside it, and a split view with one tab left ends. `session.json` stores each space's split view as tab indexes.

#### Windows

File ▸ New Window (⌘N) opens another main window on the active window's space, cascaded from it at its size, or centered at 1200×800 while it is in full screen, with no tab selected and the New Tab palette open. Each main window has an empty unified toolbar, which gives the title bar the standard height of the system's apps and centers the traffic light buttons on the sidebar's top row, and each small window an empty compact one, which keeps its title bar and row thin, with half the page margin below the row; and hides it in full screen, showing it again, with the sidebar's top row back beside the traffic lights, when the window fails to enter full screen; AppKit's window tabbing and window restoration are off. Spaces, favorites, tabs, folders, split views, the closed list, and downloads belong to the app, and the sidebar's width is the same in every window. Each window has its own current space, selected tab, sidebar visibility, library, internal page, page fullscreen, command palette, find bar, renaming, region capture, tab switcher order, docked developer tools, preview panel, page reader, question popover, and the prompt cards over the panes on its screen. Two windows can show the same space. A window shows its selected tab and, in a split view, the panes whose tabs no other window shows, because a page's view has one superview. Selecting a tab that another window shows, from a sidebar row, the palette, the switcher, a ⌘-number, Next or Previous Tab, or a favorite, moves the tab: the other window first selects its most recent tab that no window shows, or shows the empty page, or leaves the pane with a placeholder that has Show Here, which moves the tab back, and the pane's close control. Switching spaces, the selection after a tab closes, a new window, and restore never take a tab that another window shows. Page commands act on the browser window that last became main, also while Settings or History is key; sidebar commands act on the last main window, or open one while none is open. File ▸ Close Window (⇧⌘W) closes a window and keeps its tabs, and ⌘W in a window with no tab selected closes it while another browser window is open. Closing the last browser window quits, unless Settings, History, or the tab close log keeps the app running; a Dock click, the global shortcut, History's Open, or a link then opens a main window as the last one was. A window's hidden title, which the Window menu and Mission Control show, is its selected tab's title, or its space's name with no tab selected; with two or more Personas, it names the window's Persona and current space (Engines, Personas, and cookies). `session.json` keeps the open main windows, front to back, each with its ID, frame outside full screen, space, selected tab, and sidebar visibility; frames save as windows move and resize, and after the last window closes, `session.json` keeps that window. At launch the windows open back to front, so that the front one becomes key, each on its selected tab, or on its space's first tab that no other window shows when that tab has closed. A window that was in full screen opens at its frame from before, a frame that reaches less than 100 points across or down onto every screen opens centered on the main screen at its size, at most the screen's, and a file from before windows were saved opens one window centered at 1200×800 on its selection.

#### Small windows

While Settings ▸ General allows it, a link that another app sends while Arch is in the background, or less than 0.5 seconds after Arch became active, opens in a small window, which comes forward alone: 900×640 and at least 480×360, the first centered on the screen with the pointer and later ones cascaded from the last. Its one row, on the line of the traffic light buttons, has the page's icon and site, which open the palette to edit the address, then back, forward, reload or stop, share, and Move to Main Window, and the page below it lies over the window material with the tint of the tab's space: the last main window's current space when the small window opens, or the last closed main window's, or the first space, in that space's Persona, with the default engine. That space's list, ⌘-numbers, the tab switcher, the palette, Close Other Tabs, the selection after a tab closes, and archiving leave the tab out. Find, prompt cards, page fullscreen, the docked developer tools, the page reader, the agent panel, the question popover, the preview panel, and the palette work in the small window, but its palette lists no tabs; sidebar commands act on the last main window, which comes forward, and ⌃Tab goes to the page. A page that the small window's page or preview opens, and the preview's Open in Tab, open in another small window. Move to Main Window, from the button or Window ▸ Move to Main Window (⌥⌘N), puts the tab at the end of its space's open tabs and selects it in the last main window, which comes forward with the page focused; the small window closes, and the page moves without loading again. Closing a small window closes its tab into the closed list, after the page agrees, and a small window closes with its tab, also when its page closes itself with `window.close()` (on Chromium, closing at once is unverified in the app: I.14), which cancels a print or save panel on it; a download's save panel opens as a panel of its own over a small window. `session.json` keeps no small windows; their tabs are saved as open tabs of their spaces. Links that arrive during launch or while Arch is active open as tabs of the main window.

#### Engines, Personas, and cookies

Each tab has its own engine: tabs from the palette, favorites, and other apps' links use the default engine, tabs a page opens use that page's engine, and `session.json` (snapshot version 16) records each tab's ID, engine, and that engine's restorable page state, each open tab's suggested title, each pinned tab's saved page, each folder's parent folder, and each live folder's configuration with its tabs' items. It also records the Personas and the Persona of each space, favorite, and tab record; a file from before version 9 loads into the default Persona, `Persona.defaultID` (`D59DAF4B-E372-4AF9-890E-455B8B3860F3`). Personas partition the sidebar: every space, favorite, tab, and closed-tab record belongs to one, a tab keeps its Persona for life, and each Persona has its own profile in each engine and its own plugins. New Persona…, Rename Persona…, and Delete Persona… in the space menu and the Spaces menu ask with a sheet on the window; an empty name changes nothing, and New Persona adds a Persona with one space named Personal and shows that space. With the default Persona alone, nothing names Personas. With two or more, the bottom bar groups the space symbols by Persona with a 1-point divider between groups and gives each symbol its space as "Work / Research" for its tooltip and accessibility label, so two Personas' spaces named Personal are told apart, and the interface names a Persona with the space, as "Work / Research" (Persona rule 7 in `docs/windows-personas.md`): each space's header reads "Work / Research" on one line at one size, the Persona's name in regular secondary text, the slash in tertiary, and the space's name in semibold primary, with the Persona's name truncating before the space's name in a narrow sidebar, and only the space's name renames; the window title ends with " — Work / Research", the window's Persona and current space, or is "Work / Research" alone with no tab selected; the Clear Site Data card says "…in the Persona of “Work / Research”…" with the tab's space, or for a favorite's tab, which has no space, with the window's current space; and Delete Persona… asks "Delete the Persona of “Work / Research”?" with the space menu's space or the sidebar target's current space. `BrowserSession.displayedPlace(ofPersona:space:)` builds the name. The Persona pickers of Settings ▸ Plugins and the import sheet, which show no space, list Personas alone, and the Rename Persona field holds the name alone. The favorites grid shows the window's Persona's favorites and cross-fades when the window moves to another Persona's space; swipes and Next and Previous Space cross into the next group. A tab moves only to a space of its Persona: another Persona's space symbol does not light up under a dragged tab and refuses its drop. Move Left, Move Right, and dragging a symbol stay within the space's group, Delete Space is disabled for a Persona's last space, and New Space adds the space at the end of the window's Persona's group. The palette and the tab switcher list only tabs of the window's Persona, and palette commands run through its plugins. A page that a page or a preview opens joins a space of the page's Persona: the window's current space, or else the last main window's while it belongs to that Persona, or else the Persona's first space; Open Link in New Tab from a favorite's page does the same. Delete Persona…, disabled for the default Persona, says what goes and asks first. Then every main window on the Persona's spaces moves to the first remaining Persona's first space, its tabs close into the closed list without asking their pages, which closes its small windows, its unfinished downloads stop and the pages kept for them close, its spaces, folders, split views, and favorites go, its browsers close, its plugins stop, its web extensions leave the engines, and the Web Store's update results for it go, and its ID joins `removedPersonaIDs`, which saves at once. Its runtimes stay without tabs until quit, because download rows refer to their profiles. A reopened tab whose space is gone opens in its Persona's preferred space, or in the sidebar target's current space once its Persona is gone. At the next launch, before any engine starts, the session deletes the Persona's `Plugins/<UUID>` folder, its plugins' Keychain items, and its `CookieSync/<UUID>.json`, and has both engines remove its storage; the ID stays listed until both report removal, and its plugin data goes first. Cookies are copied between the two engines' profiles of one Persona, never to another Persona's, so a site stays signed in, and signed out, on both engines: into an engine when it starts after the other and before a tab switches to it, from WebKit as its cookie store reports changes, and from either engine after its pages finish loading. Copies run one at a time. A copy carries what the source changed since the two stores last agreed, a record `CookieSync` keeps: the cookies the source added or changed, and the deletion of those it removed, so a sign-out in one engine signs the other out too. A cookie only the target changed stays and travels with the next copy the other way, so an older value never replaces a newer one; when both engines changed one cookie, the source's version wins, but a deletion leaves a cookie the target changed. The record outlasts the launch in `CookieSync/<persona id>.json`: each copy that changes the record rewrites the file away from the main thread, quitting writes it once more, marked as written at quit, and waits for those writes, and the first copy of a launch reads the file. So once both engines run, a cookie that one of them deleted while the other was not running goes from the other too, and one that it changed keeps its newer value in both. A file without the mark counts as none: an engine can commit its cookie store to disk later than the file, Chromium only from time to time, so after a crash or a forced quit the file can list a cookie that one store lost, and the next launch's first copy would delete it from the other store as well. The file keeps no cookie values: for each agreed cookie with an expiry, its name, domain, path, and a SHA-256 digest of each store's version of its value, flags, and expiry to the whole second, as WebKit keeps it. A cookie and WebKit's copy of it have one digest; versions that each engine took from a site on its own can have expiries less than a second apart on either side of a whole second, and so two digests. Cookies without an expiry stay out, because both engines drop them at quit. A record read from the file counts only while each store still holds at least half of the cookies it lists that either store holds; a store that lost more, as when its profile was deleted or Chromium could not decrypt its cookies, would otherwise sign the other engine out too. Without a record, the first copy writes the cookies the target lacks or holds with other contents and deletes none. The cross-launch part of regression check 53 has not run from the bundle. A copy keeps each cookie's `SameSite` policy as far as the target store holds it: WebKit keeps Lax and Strict but no difference between `SameSite=None` and no policy, both of which it sends with requests from other sites, so such a WebKit cookie reaches Chromium as `SameSite=None` when it is secure and with no policy (Lax to Chromium) otherwise. A copy writes only the cookies the target lacks or holds with another value, flag, or expiry, so a Chromium cookie without a policy that comes back from WebKit unchanged keeps no policy in Chromium; one that a WebKit tab changes reaches Chromium as `SameSite=None` when it is secure.

#### Saving and restoring

Tabs keep their IDs across launches: a restored or reopened tab comes back under its saved ID. A restored tab of a space gets its page only as a window first shows it, as its selection or a split view pane, and then loads at its saved address, engine, and page state; until then an `UnloadedWebContents` stands in for the page with the record's address, title, engine, and state, which saving writes back unchanged, and shows the site's `/favicon.ico` in its row (Decisions); the row's icon and title are dimmed to half opacity until the page loads, so the sidebar shows which pages are open. A favorite's tab waits for its first click. So launching starts pages, and on Chromium renderers, only for the tabs the restored windows show. The session saves half a second after the last change to what `session.json` keeps, and after each finished load; a tab's loading state, icon, sound, and crash notice save nothing. The snapshot is taken on the main thread, and `SessionStore` encodes and writes it on a queue of its own; quitting writes it before the app ends.
