67. Tabs' prompt cards, on both engines: `alert`, `confirm`, and `prompt` from the console each show as a card hanging from just above the page frame, headed by the page's host, with the page's words in their own box; Return and Escape answer them, and the page under the dimming takes no click, scroll, or pinch. A camera request's card keeps Allow dimmed for half a second. Clicking another tab's row hides the card, and the first tab's row shows the waiting mark; going back shows the card at once, with text typed into a `prompt` field still there. The sidebar's Reload answers a page dialog with its safe answer. With two panes in split view, each pane shows its own tab's card and only the focused pane's card has a blue default button. HTTP authentication from a local server that answers 401 with a Basic challenge shows the login card, whose password field turns on secure input, and 1Password's ⌘\ fills it. ⌘Q with two cards open quits at once. The cards look right in light and dark appearance and with Increase Contrast, slide in and out with motion and only fade with Reduce Motion, and VoiceOver reads the card's kind and asker, then its title, and does not move into the page under it. In a small window at its smallest size, 480×360, a 420-point card fits. An `alert` that arrives while the page fills the window in fullscreen on Chromium ends the fullscreen and shows its card. Each card is only as tall as its content: `alert('a')` leaves the page visible below it, and `alert('line\n'.repeat(100))` scrolls the page's words in a box about 180 points tall with the buttons right below it. `prompt('c', 'Ada')` starts with Ada selected; after typing Grace and OK, the card leaves with Grace still in its field. With `setTimeout(() => Notification.requestPermission(), 3000)` on an https page and ⌘L typed into within those 3 seconds, the palette keeps the keys under the arriving card, Escape closes only the palette, the card then takes the focus, and Settings ▸ Websites remembers nothing; Escape pressed within half a second of a permission card taking the focus does nothing. With a link preview open from Shift-hover, `setTimeout(() => confirm('x'), 3000)` in the tab under it shows no card until the panel closes, and typing in the preview's page keeps going. With the sidebar hidden and a card showing, renaming a tab in the floating sidebar keeps the focus in the name field. In a split view of two panes in an 800-point window, each pane's close control stays clear of its card and the card's site icon; with B's `prompt('c')` field focused, clicking A's row in the sidebar moves the keys away from B's card, so Return does not answer it. Closing a Chromium favorite whose page shows an `alert` closes it, through the leave-page card when the page has a `beforeunload` handler; with that card waiting behind another selected tab, closing the tab again from its row selects it and shows the card. A login that an image from another site asks for (`<img src="http://localhost:8000/401">` on another host) puts the focus on the card, not in User Name, and http with Digest shows no lock. An `alert` from an `<iframe>` of another host on the page, as `http://localhost:<port>/` inside `http://127.0.0.1:<port>/`, shows "An embedded page on “localhost” says" above its words in the card headed by the page's host, and VoiceOver names localhost as their author, also when it announces the card while the palette is open, while the page's own `alert` has no such line. An `alert` from an empty frame that the `localhost` frame adds, `document.body.appendChild(document.createElement('iframe')).contentWindow.alert('x')`, shows "An embedded page says" on Chromium and "An embedded page on “localhost” says" on WebKit. On WebKit, a page that starts a navigation to a local port that never answers and then calls `prompt('x')` gets a card headed by its own host, not the address it loads.

On both engines, a pinned tab that follows a link to another site shows, under the pointer, a back button before its close button with the saved address in its tooltip, and the button returns to the saved page; following a link within the saved site shows no back button, while Tab ▸ Go Back to Saved Page and the row's Go Back to Saved Page are enabled. After Use Current Page as Saved Page and a relaunch, Go Back to Saved Page returns to the new saved page. Edit Saved Address… with `two words` beeps and keeps the saved address, and with `example.org` saves `https://example.org`. Copy Address pastes the row's address, and Duplicate opens a copy right after the tab, in its folder, selected, at the same address. On a favorite's tab that left the favorite's page, also through a timed `location` change on a local page while another tab is selected, the tile's menu shows Go Back to Saved Page, which returns there, and Use Current Page as Saved Page moves the favorite and its tile's icon to the current page, and is disabled while another favorite has that address; Edit Favorite… renames the favorite and refuses another favorite's address with a beep. File ▸ Import from Browser… run again after one of the pinned tabs it brought moved to another site adds no second tab for that tab's saved page.
69. On both engines, ⌘W on a selected pinned tab that left its saved page keeps its row, with its icon and title dimmed and no minus button under the pointer, selects another tab, and adds no Tab Close Log entry; clicking the row loads the saved page, and ⌘Z does not bring the old page back. The row's minus button and an extension's `tabs.remove` do the same, and a page's `window.close()` in a pinned tab unloads it and selects the tab of the page that opened it. Delete Tab removes the row, and ⌘Z reopens it pinned in its folder at its saved page. A pinned tab whose saved page is a file downloads it when clicked and keeps its row; with two such pinned tabs as a space's only tabs, clicking one downloads its file once and leaves the window on the empty page without loading the other, and in a space of pinned web pages only, ⌘W on each in turn unloads them all without loading another. With a sign-in popup that a pinned tab opened selected in its place, a critical memory pressure keeps the pinned tab loaded, and the popup's `window.close()` returns to it. With `ARCHUnloadAfterHours` at 1, a pinned WebKit tab with three pages in its back list and a Chromium favorite's tab, left unshown past the archive timer an hour later, unload, the pinned tab's row dimmed and the favorite's icon dimmed without its dot; selecting the WebKit tab returns to the same page with its back list, and clicking the favorite loads its last address on Chromium. A pinned tab that plays audio stays loaded. `memory_pressure -S -l critical` unloads every tab that no window shows except, on each engine, a tab with text typed into a field, a tab in a video call with its camera on, a tab of a site allowed to post notifications, a tab with its developer tools open, and a tab whose video, muted from the start or paused, is in picture in picture, whose window stays open; a Chromium tab whose page has a `beforeunload` handler and an edited form stays loaded without a card. Delete Persona of a test Persona puts its pinned tabs in the closed list with the reason Persona in the Tab Close Log. Settings ▸ Sidebar shows the unload stepper with its footer below the archive stepper.
70. In a space with pinned and open tabs, New Folder, then New Folder Inside three times, makes folders four levels deep, each row and its tabs indented 12 points further, and New Folder Inside is disabled on the fourth; a new folder opens its name field even inside a collapsed folder, which expands. Each folder lists its folders before its tabs. Collapsing the top folder hides everything inside it but the selected tab, which shows one level in from it, and ⌘-numbers and Next Tab pass over the hidden tabs. A tab's Move to Folder lists the pinned list's folders as the tree nests them, then the open list's after a divider: a folder that holds listed folders is a submenu whose first item moves into it, except the tab's own folder, and any other folder is an item; a folder's lists neither itself nor the folders inside it, and leaves out folders where it would pass the fourth level. Move Out of Folder moves a tab and a folder up one level, and Add to New Folder on a tab in a folder makes the new folder inside that folder. Delete Folder on a folder that holds a folder and tabs leaves that folder in its place and the tabs first among its parent's tabs. Delete Space moves the space's nested folders to its neighbor with their tree. Dragging a folder lifts its row without the rows inside it, and in a list scrolled to its end, starting the drag of a large expanded folder moves no row: over the upper third of a folder's row at each level the rows make room for it before that folder, over a tab's row in a folder it goes last among that folder's folders, and over the rest of a folder's row that row lights up and takes it on release, the lifted row shrinking into it; over its own parent's row it goes last there. Over a folder inside itself, a place past the fourth level, a place inside a collapsed folder, and the other list's rows nothing moves, and the row returns to its slot. A folder released over another space's symbol of its Persona moves there with its folders and tabs and stays pinned or open, the window following its selected tab; another Persona's symbol does not light up. A tab dropped on a folder row at the third level joins that folder. Releasing a drag neither collapses nor expands the dragged folder. The tree, the collapsed folders, and each tab's folder survive a relaunch.

## Engine Plan

| Engine | Role | Work location |
|---|---|---|
| Chromium through CEF (`ARCHEngineChromium`) | Primary engine, embedded beside the sidebar in Alloy style, which CEF uses on macOS whenever the browser has a parent view; Chrome style would need a CEF Views window per tab. Arch's engine runs Chromium-format extensions in Alloy style browsers through an extension runtime API with the app's windows as client windows (VI.1, `chrome-style-extensions.md` in `arch-chromium`). The app pins a release of `taeyoon0137/arch-chromium`, which builds CEF from source: `arch-8078-6`, CEF 156.0.58 on Chromium 156.0.8078.48, with the engine changes below, built with the H.264 and AAC codecs. Its renderer, GPU, and utility processes run in the macOS sandbox (Decisions). | This Mac, with `arch/build.sh` and `arch/release.sh` in `arch-chromium` |
| WebKit (`ARCHEngineWebKit`) | Secondary and debugging engine. It uses the system framework and needs no source download. | This Mac |

- Remaining engine gaps are queued in Phase II. Gaps that need engine changes, such as Chromium-format extensions, are queued in Phase VI. They are made in `taeyoon0137/arch-chromium`, whose build needs about 150GB of free disk space and a full Xcode installation, and reach the app as a release that `scripts/setup_cef.sh` pins.
- Chromium's password, card, and address storage stay disabled. Apple Passwords handles passwords and passkeys; 1Password handles cards and addresses.
- Browsing history is engine-independent and lives in the `History` module; the engine protocols keep none.
- You may change CEF or WebKit when a feature needs it. Phase VI lists the planned changes. Each CEF change is one commit on `arch-8078` in `arch-chromium` and one row below, with its task and the first release that carries it; the repository's documents and build and release scripts are not listed.

## Work Queue

### How to use the queue

Each task carries the reasoning it needs, so that work can be split across models by capability:

- `high`: a design choice, a change to the Objective-C++ bridge or the engine protocols, a change to the `session.json` schema, window architecture, or threading. Use the most capable model available.
- `medium`: a view or session method inside an existing module that follows an existing pattern (for example `DownloadsView` or `togglePinned`). A mid-tier model can take it with the files and the done condition below.
- `low`: a mechanical edit, a document update, or a verification by checklist. Any model.
- `user`: a step only the user can take, such as approving Arch in another app, opening a pull request outside this repository, signing in to a tool, or answering a macOS prompt. Models must not take this step: an agent lists it in its final report, and dependent rows wait for it.

Rules for every tier:

1. Take tasks at or below your tier. Read the files the task names before editing, and keep to them. If the task needs a file, a protocol member, or a decision it does not list, stop, write the blocker into the task row (`blocked: …`), and leave the code unchanged.
2. Respect the order within a phase and the dependencies in the task text. Phases I and II run before III to V and VII; VI is independent, and VII builds on V.11.
3. One task per commit with the `Task(<ID>):` subject, staged by path. Run `swift build` for every code change and `scripts/bundle_app.sh` plus a launch when the task says "from the bundle".
4. Finish by updating this file (remove the row, update Current State) and the feature row in `docs/features.md` in the same commit, then go through the After each task list in `AGENTS.md`.
5. Verification steps use a fresh Application Support folder unless the step says otherwise.

### Branches waiting to merge

Bookmarks, agent tabs, and script hooks now share the same sidebar and event source. The CEF distribution is pinned to `arch-8078-6`. The outstanding branches below are local working copies under `.claude/worktrees/`.

1. `work/merge-design` contains the sidebar, window chrome, palette, page tools, sheets, Settings, and menu design pass. Preserve the current prompt card behavior when reconciling its changes.
2. `work/sync` holds the sync work through V.9 part 5 and local changes for part 6. Reconcile its older session schema with the current bookmark and Persona records.

The engine repository keeps an unfinished start of VI.10 on its local branch `wip/writing-tools-in-place` (see that row).

### Phase II: Chromium engine gaps

## Decisions

- History visits: one visit per page change per tab. A reload, a repeated load of the same URL in the same tab, and the initial load of a restored tab do not add visits. The palette ranks by visit count, so pages that reload often would otherwise crowd out typed destinations. A hash change counts as a visit because its address is one the user may revisit.
- Permissions and dialogs default to the safe answer in the protocol: deny and dismiss. Feature code shows the user interface.
- `ARCHEngine` keeps only members that work: each has a caller in feature code or is a report that an adapter makes, except the two page script members that the plugins' context will call (V.11). A member comes in with the feature that needs it, as a theme color, an engine's version, a profile's name, or the forward list would; until then the engines do not read what no feature shows, so Chromium's helper reads no `theme-color` meta. An application names a profile by its storage alone (`createBrowserContext(storageIdentifier:)`), because nothing looks a profile up by another name. No feature makes a profile that keeps nothing on disk, so the WebKit adapter's tests make one through an internal call of the adapter rather than through the protocol. `Engine.capabilities` names only `extensions`, the one capability that feature code must know of before an engine starts; feature code tests an optional member, such as `devTools`, directly.
- Downloads save with an unused name to the downloads folder chosen in Settings. A save panel appears only when Settings asks where to save each download.
- A WebKit download's pause, resume, and cancel are decided in `WebKitDownloadLifecycle`, a value type whose decisions the download item carries out on WebKit's objects, because a test cannot make a `WKDownload`; the tests meet it with WebKit's reports in any order. A resume while WebKit still stops the paused download waits for the resume data and then restarts from it, since resuming at once would restart from the beginning, and a stop that then brings no data would cancel the restarted download. When that stop brings no data, the waiting resume restarts the download from the start, which chooses the destination again, so the save panel opens when Settings asks where to save; a pause without resume data cancels the download only when no resume waits. A finish completes the download even after a pause or a cancel, since WebKit reports it only for a whole file. The find result and the context menu's tag filter are static functions the tests call with counts and with menus they build, and a dialog's single answer is tested on a web contents that loads no page.
- The page context menu holds only items both engines can run. Look Up, Share, Learn and Ignore Spelling, saving or copying an image, media controls, and Undo stay out on both; they can come back as app features that work on both engines (IV.28 holds the design for macOS's text items).
- AppKit adds its own items to the page context menu on both engines, the way it does in any app: AutoFill, Services, and Writing Tools where macOS offers it. They come from showing the menu with `NSMenu.popUpContextMenu(_:with:for:)` for a view that offers them, which WebKit does itself and the Chromium adapter does through `EngineContextMenuPresenter`, as Chromium's own context menu code shows its menu, naming the view that gets the items WebKit's view gets: Chromium's page view in a field while it has a text input context, a requestor for a link, and CEF's container view, which offers nothing, elsewhere; no public API builds them, and `popUp(positioning:at:in:)` adds none. They never enter the menu's items, so neither the tab nor a test sees them, and AppKit decides from each engine's view which to add. The parity target is the menu a WebKit tab shows in Arch, not WebKit's own: the WebKit adapter keeps only WebKit's five editing and spelling items, and WebKit's other items can be told apart only by WebCore tags above those that `WebUIDelegate.h` documents, which the rule against relying on undocumented behavior keeps out. Writing Tools on Chromium is AppKit's: no Writing Tools coordinator is set on Chromium's page view, whose delegate would need the page text's geometry and previews that CEF's API does not give (VI.10), and no menu items are added by hand, which would show twice where AppKit adds its own. A password field on Chromium gets AutoFill through Arch's engine (VI.9): the bridge's call around a field's menu gives Chromium's page view a text input context in a password field, and the view offers the field's text to no service meanwhile, so the presenter names the page view as for any field and needs no other sign of a password field; input methods stay off in password fields at all other times, as Chromium keeps them.
- Quitting first answers every tab prompt, so every engine callback has its answer before CEF shuts down at `NSApplicationWillTerminateNotification`; AppKit still stops a quit for a window-level system sheet with more than one button. Answering at `willTerminate` instead would come after the engines needed their answers. A page dialog stops its page, so a navigation the user starts answers the dialog first on both engines; CEF resets its dialog state as soon as that navigation starts, so the end of loading, not the reset, ends the suppression of new dialogs.
- A tab's prompts show as Arch's cards over the tab's pane, not as AppKit sheets: a sheet blocked the whole window, `endSheet` ran the run loop for about 0.27 seconds, and a window could show only one sheet, which needed the deferral of the others. Window-level confirmations, such as Delete Persona, a Persona's name, the quit confirmations, and the offer to become the default browser, stay system alerts and sheets. A page's file chooser waits among the tab's prompts but shows Arch's open or save panel as a sheet, since AppKit's panels are windows of their own; it ends with Cancel when its tab leaves the screen rather than waiting to show again.
- The card's surface is the opaque window background, as the find bar's, the preview panel's, the question popover's, and the panes' close control's are, because a material over the engine's view cannot sample it. `PromptSurface` holds it alone, so a material that a screen check finds working changes only that file.
- A page cannot pass a card off as the app's: the card hangs from 3 points above the page frame in the window's margin and comes down from behind that line, its asker comes from the tab's address and never from page text, the sidebar marks the tab that waits, and a card for a page in fullscreen ends the fullscreen first, since fullscreen has no margin or frame.
- Return and Escape belong to a card's buttons only while the card has the window's focus, and only then is its default button drawn as the prominent one, so Return in a sidebar field never answers a prompt.
- A card counts the focus as its page's only in the tab's engine view and in the fields over the page that report their focus, never by where a view lies: the palette, the preview panel, and the floating sidebar lie over the pane, and a card that took their keys would answer with Return or Escape under them.
- A button that grants something waits half a second after its card shows, its tab comes back, or its window becomes key, so a key or click meant for the page cannot press it. Escape waits as long on such a card, because its cancel answer is remembered too.
- Escape on an armed permission card is Don't Allow and is remembered, as the standard cancel button is; only a card that ended without the user, as when its tab closed or Arch quit, leaves nothing remembered.
- Browsing depends on Extensions, so Extensions asks its questions as an interface-free `WebExtensionQuestion`, and the session's web extension host turns it into a card.
- A card ends a page's fullscreen only where Arch fills the window with the page, as on Chromium: WebKit's `exitFullscreen()` also ends the page's other media presentations, picture in picture among them, so a WebKit page in its own fullscreen window keeps it and the card waits behind, as a sheet did.
- Calling a DevTools protocol method on a Chromium tab attaches a client until the browser closes. While attached, the client makes Chromium ignore an unresponsive renderer, stopping the `beforeunload` and unload timeouts; a hung page with a `beforeunload` handler cannot be closed. Features use the protocol only when CEF has no other route. The History menu therefore calls the page's `history.go`, first stopping any main frame navigation in progress because Chromium ignores page history requests during it. The menu has no effect on hung or crashed pages, pages that cannot run scripts (such as those served with a CSP sandbox), or pages that replace `History.prototype.go`; Back and Forward still work.
- Clearing or deleting history, from the History menu or the History window, changes the visit store only. Neither engine lets the app remove entries from a tab's back-forward list, so open tabs keep their earlier pages in Back, Forward, and the History menu until they close. The store keeps one row per address with its last visit. As a result, the History window lists each page once, under the day of its last visit, and clearing a range removes pages last visited in it, including their earlier visits.
- Pages keep each engine's own default styles. Of the differences between them, only two are worth evening out from the app: WebKit draws Korean, Japanese, and Chinese pages that set no font in a serif face where Chromium uses a sans-serif one, and its field placeholders are lighter (`#A9A9A9` against `#757575`). Chromium already uses the better value in both cases, and WebKit could take an app style sheet only through a private API or a `<style>` element that a strict CSP blocks and reports, so the app adds none.
- The History menu and Back show each engine's own back-forward list. WebKit combines script navigations without a user gesture into one entry, so a WebKit tab can list fewer pages than a Chromium tab on the same site.
- Engine choice is per tab. A tab opened by a page inherits that page's engine; other new tabs use the default engine; the user can switch a tab's engine at any time. Cookies are shared by copying between the engines' stores of one Persona: a copy carries the cookies the source added, changed, or deleted since the stores last agreed, and site storage such as local storage stays per engine. Clear Site Data is the exception: it removes the site's cookies from both stores together while the copies wait. The record of what the stores agreed on outlasts the launch in a file per Persona, so what one engine changed while the other was not running reaches the other too. The file keeps each cookie's name, domain, and path with a digest of its contents but no value, because Chromium encrypts the values it stores and a plain copy beside them would undo that. A record from the file counts only while both stores hold most of its cookies, because a store that lost its cookies, as after Chromium's Keychain key changed, would otherwise carry the loss to the other store as deletions. For the same reason, a file from a launch that did not quit counts as none: after a crash, a store can lack cookies that the file lists as agreed.
- Chromium's renderer, GPU, and utility processes run in the macOS sandbox. The helper initializes CEF's sandbox context (`CefScopedSandboxContext`, which loads `libcef_sandbox.dylib` from the framework next to the helper bundles) before it loads the framework, and the runtime leaves CEF's `no_sandbox` off, so a page that takes over its renderer process stays inside Chromium's sandbox profile. The first Chromium build had turned the sandbox off without recording a reason. Check 57 covers what the sandbox could refuse.
- Chromium tabs play H.264 and AAC media, such as MP4 videos: `arch-chromium` builds CEF with `proprietary_codecs=true` and `ffmpeg_branding=Chrome` (`arch/common.sh`), from `arch-8078-4` on. Earlier releases played only royalty-free formats such as VP9, so MP4 videos failed on Chromium tabs. A build that leaves this Mac needs the codecs' licensing reviewed first.
- Use private APIs only behind flags on `flags://`: one for all private APIs, one for each API, and one for each feature that uses one. Check each API's availability quietly, in the background when it is registered or just before its first use. If a feature's API is missing or switched off, the feature falls back to public behavior or stays hidden. You may fork CEF or WebKit when a feature needs it.
- Features use macOS facilities by default on both engines so Arch behaves like other Mac apps. For example, Chromium's picture in picture uses AVKit rather than Chromium's own window.
- Out of scope: a browser-side password manager; page translation, unless an engine offers it to embedding apps; and features specific to one browser rather than common to browsers, such as per-site CSS and JavaScript and notes. App icon choice is not offered for now; whether to add sound effects is undecided.
- A Persona owns its spaces and keeps cookies, sessions, site storage, and installed plugins separate. Favorites stay alive across spaces within their Persona. The default Persona keeps each engine's default storage (WebKit's default data store and CEF's global request context), so the upgrade to Personas moved no site data and signed nobody out; every other Persona has storage of its own named by its UUID. Cookies copy between the engines only within a Persona. A tab or space never moves to another Persona, because its pages would lose their storage. A deleted Persona's stored data goes at the next launch, before any engine starts, because an engine cannot remove a profile it has loaded, and only for the IDs that `session.json` lists in `removedPersonaIDs`, never because a Persona is missing.
- Every piece of state has exactly one owner. State that can differ between two windows open at once, such as what a window shows, focuses, or edits, belongs to that window's `BrowserWindowModel`. State bound to one Persona's engine storage (its contexts, browsers, cookie copying, plugin manager, and web extension runtime) belongs to its `PersonaEngines`, from first use until quit. The engine applications, CEF initialization, crash totals, and the primary engine belong to the session's engine section. Everything else the user owns (Personas, spaces, favorites, tabs, the closed list, downloads and the pages kept open for them, each stored with the runtime that created it, permissions, zoom, and persistence) belongs to `BrowserSession`. `BrowserSession.swift` keeps the session's members in one file, in sections by area, because most serve several areas and the lists they change keep their setters private to that file. A part that stands on its own, such as the prompt queue (`TabPromptQueue`), cookie copying (`CookieSync`), the answers to permission requests (`SitePermissionAnswers`), and download notifications (`DownloadNotifier`), is a separate type owned by the session.
- At most one window shows a tab, because an engine's view has one superview. A window shows its selected tab and, in a split view, those of that tab's other panes that no other window shows. Explicit selection moves a tab: a sidebar click, a palette tab row, the tab switcher, ⌘-numbers, Next and Previous Tab, a favorite's tab, a tab's own request (a question it needs to show, or the return button of its picture-in-picture window), and selection by sync or a plugin. The window that shows the tab first gives it up, selecting its fallback when the tab is its selected tab or leaving the pane otherwise, and only then does the selecting window run its own side effects, so the window losing the tab lets go of its docked developer tools before the other docks them. Automatic selection never takes a tab that another window shows: switching spaces, the fallback after a tab closes, restore, a new window, and the losing window's fallback. With no free tab, the window shows the empty page.
- A restored tab gets its page only when a window first shows it, as its selection or a split view pane (`BrowserWindowModel.updateShownTabs()` calls `BrowserSession.loadPage(of:)`), so launching with many saved tabs creates pages, and on Chromium renderers, only for the tabs the windows show. Until then `Tab.webContents` holds a stand-in, `UnloadedWebContents`, rather than nil, and `Tab.isLoaded` tells the two apart: most readers of `Tab.webContents`, the window's commands among them, reach only tabs that windows show, which have their pages, so an optional would give each of them a branch for a case it never meets, while the stand-in answers every member as a tab without a page does. Saving writes the record's address, title, engine, and page state back unchanged, and closing needs no agreement and closes no page. Zoom, page scripts, and reloads, Clear Site Data's among them, wait for the page, which takes its host's zoom and the plugins' scripts of the moment it loads; find has no matches, media plays nothing, and reading, scripts, captures, and PDF fail. A load replaces the saved page, so the tab loads that address from the start, and the engine switch, which the site settings popover offers for a shown tab only, does nothing. Neither engine owns or lists the stand-in, so web extensions see such a tab once its page loads, and the session's window report leaves it out, so that an index among a window's tabs counts only the tabs the engines know. The stand-in shows the site's `/favicon.ico`, loaded through `FaviconLoader` without cookies, so that the row keeps an icon until the page reports its own. Favorites' tabs keep waiting as their saved records (`savedFavoriteTabs`), because a favorite has no tab until it opens.
- Code comments and documents cite the window rules, the Persona rules, and the open decisions of the multiple windows design by number, as in "window rule 2". `docs/windows-personas.md` numbers them under Decisions and Open decisions.
- Pinned tabs, favorites, and their folders are Arch's bookmarks. Arch keeps no separate bookmark store, bookmarks bar, or manager window: one place to organize them is enough, import and sync already treat them as bookmarks, the palette finds them, and nothing sits above the page. A pinned tab saves the page it was pinned at. Closing it unloads it back to that page instead of removing it, because a bookmark should outlast its page. Delete Tab removes it. The row offers a way back only when the page leaves the saved site, because pinned web apps change their addresses constantly (`docs/bookmarks.md`, bookmark rules 1 to 4).
- Pinned tabs and favorites that no window shows unload after the hours set in Settings ▸ Sidebar. Every tab that no window shows unloads when macOS reports memory pressure. Both use the same `UnloadedWebContents` stand-in as restored tabs, keeping the page's address, title, and icon, and on WebKit its state: a WebKit tab returns with its back list, while a Chromium tab, whose adapter keeps no page state, loads its last address, as after a relaunch. A tab that plays media, shows a video in picture in picture, uses the camera or microphone, holds text the user typed, shows a site allowed to post notifications, waits on a prompt, keeps downloads, has its developer tools open, or belongs to an agent stays loaded; screen sharing counts only if CEF reports it as video access, which is not verified, since WebKit has no public state for it. An automatic unload never shows a leave-page card: it answers a Chromium page's question with Stay, and since WebKit cannot run `beforeunload` handlers, the typed text exclusion keeps drafts there (bookmark rule 5).
- Folders nest four levels deep, leaving room for names in the narrowest sidebar. Browser import still joins nested folder names into one level: its record finds folders by source ID, but merging by name, used when the record finds none, compares the joined names (bookmark rule 6).
- Live folders are pinned folders filled by a source: RSS and Atom feeds fetched directly over HTTPS through an ephemeral `URLSession` outside every Persona's storage, and GitHub searches through the user's own `gh`, signed in through the built-in terminal. Arch runs only `gh auth status` and `gh api graphql` with a fixed query and the search on standard input, and never reads `gh`'s sign-in. No server or account of Arch's own takes part. The source belongs to the folder in `session.json`. Items, read state, and fetch state belong to `live-folders.json` and stay on each Mac. A feed address with a query, which can carry a private feed's token, stays in the Keychain, out of `session.json` and sync. `gh` gets a minimal environment, with `GH_CONFIG_DIR` and `XDG_CONFIG_HOME` from the login shell so that it finds its own sign-in, and never a token variable. `LiveFolders` uses `AI`'s `LoginShell` and `CommandLineProcess`, made public, so one copy of the process code runs every command-line tool (`docs/bookmarks.md`, live folder rules 1 to 3 and 8).
- A live folder's items are pinned tabs whose saved page is the item, so the bookmark rules apply. Closing one dismisses the item, since items are meant to be worked through. New items are unread, as are GitHub items changed since the user last looked. An item's tab requests nothing from its item's site, its icon included, before the user opens it, so a folder of links contacts none of their sites by itself. Folders refresh at intervals with backoff and limits. GitHub folders also refresh when the user returns to Arch, because pull requests change while the user works (live folder rules 4 to 7).
- Code comments and documents cite the bookmark rules and the live folder rules by number, as in "bookmark rule 4". `docs/bookmarks.md` numbers them under Decisions.
- The app shell names the app's Application Support folder and gives it to the session and `PluginIdentifierMigration`. The session keeps its own files there (`BrowserSession.userDataDirectory`; WebKit keeps website data where the system puts it for the app) and makes the stores that keep theirs there, history, remembered permissions, and site search, which Settings, the History window, the palette, and imports reach through the session. `AIService.shared`, which views of several modules read, names the same folder for `ai.json` and `ai-usage.json`.
- The model runs on the main thread. Both adapters call every delegate method, completion handler, and change closure of the engine protocols there (`docs/engine-protocol.md`). `AI` and `Plugins` are main actor types; `BrowserSession`, `BrowserWindowModel`, and `Tab` are not isolated yet (I.18), and the comment at the top of `BrowserWindowModel.swift` says how their members reach main actor code until then. Work moved off the main thread, such as the history store's queries and the session file's writes, runs on its own queues and reports back on the main thread. With tools version 5.10, `Package.swift` builds every target in the Swift 5 language mode, in which the compiler checks calls into main actor code but not whether the values that cross threads are `Sendable`.
- Arch has its own plugin system with more capabilities than browser extensions. It includes built-in plugins for later features. WebKit extensions and Chromium-format extensions run as one kind of plugin; password managers such as 1Password come as plugins.
- A WebKit tab moves into and out of a web extension's pages by changing web contents. WebKit loads an extension's page only in a web view made from the extension context's `webViewConfiguration` and no other page there, and its documentation asks the app to swap web views in tabs. A WebKit web contents keeps its web view for life, so the adapter, which alone knows which configuration a page needs, cancels a main frame navigation across that line in its navigation policy, which WebKit asks about links, scripts, `tabs.update`, Back and Forward, and the app's loads alike, and hands its UI delegate a replacement from the same profile with the same page scripts on the next turn of the run loop, after WebKit's callback, since the delegate closes the old web contents. The session puts the replacement in the tab's place as an engine switch does. The replacement starts a back-forward list of its own, as after an engine switch, because neither kind of web view loads the other's pages, so Back does not cross the line. A replacement into an extension's pages runs the page with the extension's access, so the policy keeps Chromium's line: it makes one for the app's own loads, for a frame of the same extension, and for a page that `web_accessible_resources` names for the starting frame's origin or extension, and for no other navigation, so a web page cannot open an extension's options or other inner pages, with a query of its choosing, in its tab or a window it opens. WebKit describes the app's loads and a page's own navigations alike in `WKNavigationAction`, so the navigation controller notes the address of each load it starts, and the policy treats a main frame navigation to that address as the app's, once. WebKit has no public check for web-accessible resources, so the adapter reads the manifest's entries itself, with `WKWebExtension.MatchPattern` for their `matches`. The adapter links the replacement's extension tab to the old one, so its next window report tells the extensions `didReplaceTab(_:with:)` instead of a close and an open, and the protocols need no member for it. A tab restored at an extension's page waits for that extension to load into its profile: its web contents loads nothing, reports the page's address and saved state so that saving keeps them, and hands over a replacement that restores the state once the extension loads. It waits for nothing else and has no time limit, because extensions load only after their engine and profile start; a tab whose extension never loads, as one removed, switched off, or refused, stays blank at its address until another navigation ends the wait.
- Chromium DevTools show inside the page area. Arch's engine fills the bridge's view with the DevTools front-end, in Chromium's docked mode without a `Browser` and without the front-end's docking controls, and the app places that view beside the page or, undocked, in a window of its own, so the Developer menu's sides, the shared sizes, and the margin handle apply. The front-end's own docking, which the device toolbar and the screenshot commands require, stays off, and Workspaces folder access is declined, because the tools have no dialog host in a view. WebKit's Web Inspector opens inside the page through the private `_inspector` object behind private API flags. With them off, `isInspectable` still lets the browser bundled with macOS inspect WebKit tabs.
- AI features use providers the user connects through an API key or a command-line tool signed in through the built-in terminal. The user chooses providers per feature. Arch ships no model or provider account. All AI features, hooks, and features that AI adds at run time use one AI interface, designed in `docs/ai.md`.
- Now Playing and the media keys belong to the app on both engines. Chromium's own media controls stay off because they would use the same process-wide centers. WebKit's GPU process registers its own Now Playing client for the app. No public API turns it off, so a WebKit tab can also appear through it.
- Arch remembers permission answers without asking whether to, because a one-time choice could not hold on Chromium. Chromium's own prompt keeps every Allow and Don't Allow in the profile, and CEF's prompt callback has no one-time answer. Settings ▸ Websites changes and removes answers, and the session copies each change into Chromium's content settings. Screen recording is never remembered.
- Arch's notification permission from macOS belongs to the app bundle and stays separate from a website's answer. macOS asks only right after the user's own Allow for a website's notifications, on the card or in Settings ▸ Websites, through Turn On Notifications… there, or when a download finishes, never at launch or for a remembered Allow, and the page gets its answer before macOS asks; a refusal leaves the website's Allow in place, so the website works once the user turns notifications on. A notification the page shows while macOS's question is unanswered is therefore not shown, since the engine asks macOS for nothing before it posts. Holding the page's answer until macOS answers was rejected: Chromium would hold the tab's next permission request meanwhile, macOS's question can stay unanswered for long, so the hold would need a time limit, and the loss happens only while macOS asks, which it does once. Arch's process stays a `UserNotifications` client, and Arch's engine posts websites' notifications through `UserNotifications` too (VI.7): macOS refuses `NSUserNotificationCenter` posts from a bundle that also uses `UserNotifications`, and the old API reports no permission to tell the user about, so moving Arch's own notifications to the old API was rejected, as was a CEF API that hands notifications to the app, which would rebuild display, closing, clicks, buttons, and replies. The engine posts every notification as one the user can show as a banner or an alert, without `Arch Helper (Alerts)`, a bundle of its own whose posts would not follow Arch's permission, and without Chromium's private calls, which Arch uses only behind flags. The bundle needs no notification key: `UserNotifications` has no usage description, and `NSUserNotificationAlertStyle` and the Alerts helper's name and icon matter only to the old API.
- CEF builds from source in the separate private repository `taeyoon0137/arch-chromium` rather than in this one. It holds CEF's own history: `master` and the CEF branch mirror upstream, and Arch's changes are commits on `arch-<CEF branch>`, so moving to a new CEF branch replays them. Each release carries one distribution with its SHA-256 and its symbols, and this repository pins one release, so every app commit names the engine it builds with.
- Agents reach the running app through one Unix domain socket in `~/Library/Application Support/Arch/Agent/`. This counts as local inter-process communication, not a server under the Goals: it has no TCP or UDP port, so no web page or other machine can reach it. It exists only while Arch runs and an in-app agent session or Settings ▸ AI ▸ Agents ▸ Allow Outside Clients uses it. It accepts only Arch's bundled `arch-cli` running as the same user. Arch checks its code directory hash through the peer's audit token, as clients check Arch. A loopback port would be reachable from pages and is a server; an XPC Mach service needs a launchd job that can outlive the app. If the user reads the rule more strictly, the channel moves to a custom Apple Event class with the sender's audit token (`docs/agents.md`, Fallback transport), so `arch-cli`, `arch-mcp`, and subscription sessions keep working. `docs/agents.md` lists this reading under Open decisions for the user.
- The agents' executables are `arch-cli` and `arch-mcp`, never `arch`, because macOS ships `/usr/bin/arch`. They share one Mach-O: the `ArchCLI` target with product `arch-cli`, in `Arch.app/Contents/Helpers`. The `arch-mcp` executable is a relative symbolic link, distinguished by `argv[0]`, so the peer check uses one code directory hash. Arch adds them to PATH only through links the user requests and downloads nothing.
- A ChatGPT or Claude subscription connects only through the vendor's own command-line tool, Codex's `codex` or Claude Code's `claude`. The user installs it and signs in through the built-in terminal; Arch runs it headless as a subprocess with its own sign-in. Arch never reads a tool's credentials, uses a vendor's OAuth client, callback, or endpoint, imitates a tool, or reads a usage endpoint. Arch builds every argument: the tool's own tools, settings, hooks, plugins, other MCP servers, telemetry, and update checks are off, and only Arch's MCP server connects. Arch checks Claude Code's start event and Codex's stream and ends a session that shows anything else. Only agent sessions give command-line tools access to tools from Arch, and only Arch's (`docs/ai.md`, Command-line tools). The vendors' terms were not read; the design relies on internal use without distribution (Goals).
- Every agent access check runs in the app's tool host, which uses the same code for in-app sessions on a CLI or an API-key model, `arch-cli`, and `arch-mcp`. Approvals happen only in Arch's interface beside the page. The tools' own approvals, MCP elicitation, AppleScript, and the agent never approve. No tool, command, or MCP call changes settings, grants, site exclusions, permissions, plugins, or Personas (`docs/agents.md`, Decisions).
- Arch never changes macOS's Low Power Mode and offers no way to make Arch alone ignore it; the address bar only shows that it is on. Neither engine has a public route. Chromium 156 never reads the mode: neither Chromium nor CEF uses `isLowPowerModeEnabled` or its notification, and `base::PowerMonitor` on macOS reads only the power source, thermal state, and CPU speed limit. A Chromium tab that slows with the mode is therefore slowed by macOS, which caps apps that draw with the display at 30 frames per second in Low Power Mode on macOS 26 and later according to developer reports, not Apple's documentation, and offers no public per-app exception. WebKit's WebCore reads the mode in the web content process and halves page rendering updates, with no `WKPreferences` or other public setting to change it. The remaining routes are private APIs, swizzling in the web content process, or engine patches whose only purpose is to defeat a power policy, so they are not taken. Chromium's own Energy Saver is separate: Local State's `performance_tuning.battery_saver_mode.state`, by default `kEnabledBelowThreshold`, throttles every Chromium frame to 30 Hz, starts V8's battery saver, and freezes eligible background pages while the Mac runs on battery below 20 percent, with or without Low Power Mode. Arch keeps that default and shows nothing for it; `CefPreferenceManager::GetGlobalPreferenceManager()` can change it at run time if a feature needs to.
- The Low Power Mode indicator sits in the address bar, with the page's other state: before the share button of the sidebar's address pill, which a hidden sidebar shows once its card slides in, and of a small window's top row. It keeps the battery symbol on every Mac, as the sign macOS itself uses for the mode, but its words name the mode rather than a pane, since the pane it opens, `x-apple.systempreferences:com.apple.Battery-Settings.extension`, is named Battery on a Mac with a battery and Energy on one without (its `Info.plist` shows both names and allows the URL scheme). That identifier is undocumented; it is a URL opened by `NSWorkspace`, not a framework API, so it is not listed under Private APIs. If macOS renames it, System Settings opens at its last pane, which regression check 60 catches.
- Web extensions also install from the Web Store, Google's extension store for browsers built on Chromium: from a store page in a tab, a pasted store address or identifier, the install sheet, and on Chromium tabs the store's own install button (`docs/web-store.md`). Arch downloads the package from Google's public update endpoint only when the user acts, sends only the identifiers, versions, its engine's Chromium version, and the Mac's platform, without cookies, and stages every package for review. The store's page addresses name another browser product in their host names, which `AGENTS.md` allows only in the README's opening and in browser import, so `AGENTS.md` allows them only in `WebStore.swift`, as constants its tests also use, and in `docs/web-store.md`. The interface calls it the Web Store.
- A web extension's identifier counts as proven only by a CRX3 signature: a valid proof by the key whose digest begins with the package's `crx_id`, and, for a store install, a valid proof by the store's publisher key. A `.crx` file without a valid developer proof is refused, as in Chromium, and `plugins.json` records each install's proof in entries that name their origin with `signedSource` or `webStore` in place of `source`, so that builds before IV.26 keep them unchanged instead of dropping the proof. A package can copy any extension's public `key`, and native apps trust the identifier that the browser passes them, so only a signature distinguishes the real extension from a copy. Every proof in the header must hold, not only the developer's, and the header may not hold a `.zip` archive's end record signature, as in Chromium's verifier, so that Arch installs no `.crx` file that Chromium's own installer would refuse. A `.crx` file must hold the web extension of its identifier: its signatures prove no identifier that a script plugin's `plugin.json` declares. The publisher key's digest was confirmed against the store package in the Chromium source's test data and, on 2026-11-13, against a package that the endpoint offered (`docs/web-store.md`, Facts).
- Arch asks the Web Store about updates only when the user clicks Check for Updates in Settings ▸ Plugins and when Settings ▸ Plugins first shows a Persona in a run, and updates install only through review. No timer, launch, or background check runs, so Arch adds no update service (row 37), and the store learns which extensions a Persona has only when the user looks at them.
- Native apps install host manifests only for browsers they know, so Arch reads the host folders that `BrowserSource.nativeMessagingHostFolders` lists without changing them, at its first load per run of an extension that may message native apps, and links each valid manifest into its own `NativeMessagingHosts/` folder, where the user's own files take precedence (`docs/password-managers.md`). Links follow the vendors' changes, and both engines read this folder. A manifest found this way answers only extensions with proven identifiers, and only while it is behind Arch's own link, since a user's file takes its name. On WebKit `NativeMessaging` refuses each call of an unproven version. Chromium also reads its own system folder for a name that Arch's folder lacks, so on Chromium the gate covers every manifest there as well, valid for discovery or not; an engine change that points that folder elsewhere would remove the need. On Chromium, which reads the links itself, an unproven version leaves only the Chromium engine, as for a failure to start there, also in the other Personas before a new link appears and as a profile starts. A host whose native app does not start for Arch, such as iCloud Passwords' helper while its constraints exclude Arch, gets no link.
- `scripts/install_app.sh` copies the signed `build/Arch.app` into `/Applications` as a real bundle for native apps, such as 1Password, that accept only a signed browser there, not a link. A script follows each build in the Build Procedure, whereas copying from inside the app would replace the running bundle. It replaces only an earlier Arch or a link, keeping the earlier bundle until the new one is in place. It installs the bundle as `scripts/bundle_app.sh` signs it, with the user's team's Developer ID Application identity when the Mac has one and otherwise an Apple Development identity, and the signing changes only when a native app asks for more.
- Apple's helper for iCloud Passwords starts only for a parent with the browser passkey entitlement or for the browsers that Apple signs into its launch constraints from `apple/password-manager-resources`. IV.14 asks for the entitlement, which passkeys need anyway. Apple's list, whose pull request asks for a public website and download link, is the alternative if the entitlement is not granted, and since Arch plans no distribution (Goals), publishing Arch for it is the user's decision (IV.24). `docs/password-managers.md` keeps the entry with a placeholder for the team identifier. Arch reads the helper's constraints and matches both their pairs of signing identifier and team and their entitlement condition against its own signature; Settings ▸ Plugins shows the result, and Arch makes no link for the helper's host while it meets neither.
- Wherever the interface names a Persona, it shows "Persona / Space", such as "Work / Research", with the surface's space, also in a sheet about the whole Persona, as "the Persona of “Work / Research”" (Persona rule 7 in `docs/windows-personas.md`). Only a surface without a space, such as the Persona pickers of Settings ▸ Plugins and the import sheet, names the Persona alone. One form keeps every surface consistent: the space header, the window title, the bottom bar's tooltips, the Bookmarks menu, and the sheets and cards that act on a space or a Persona, and one helper, `BrowserSession.displayedPlace(ofPersona:space:)`, builds it for all of them. The user set this rule on 2026-11-13.
- Hooks have parts, each with one job, one interface, and its own tests (`docs/hooks.md`): one browser event bus in `Events`, shared by the AI hook center (through `BrowserHooks`), hooks, plugins' events, and outside programs; a hook registry in `Hooks` whose only action is a script; a script runner on the script plugin sandbox, with shell scripts only when the user trusts each file; one agent run API in `Agent` for scripts, Arch's own interface, and `arch-cli`; model tiers in `AI`; and an agent hook editor that writes an ordinary hook and a script the user can edit. Events carry no page text, titles, or file paths.
- Agent runs never ask the user: a step that would ask is refused. A run names a model or a tier (Fast, Standard, or Deep), whose models the user assigns once in Settings ▸ AI ▸ Model Tiers, and an empty tier fails rather than using another model. The record levels are Keep the Session, Keep Activity Only, and Keep Nothing, and none writes a prompt, reply, title, or page text to disk. The Tidy Tab Names preset runs on a new tab's first page load, because a new tab has no title when it opens, with the Fast tier, Keep Nothing, and `rename_tab` only.
- `hooks.json` is sealed like `agent.json` and holds each script's digest and the providers the user set up, so no program can add or change a hook, or send its data to a provider it wrote into `ai.json`, by editing Arch's files. Outside programs may watch events under the Watch Browser Events scope and propose hooks, which take effect only after the user allows each in Arch with Touch ID, reach no more than the proposing grant (the event's tab, titles and addresses only, no reply, every run logged), and pause when that grant ends; this is the one exception to the rule that no command changes Arch's settings. `arch-mcp` offers events as the tool `watch_events`, not as notifications, and offers no hook or agent run tool.
- One scheduler, `AIHookScheduler` in `AI`, runs the AI hook center's hooks, plugin hooks, and the hook registry's hooks, so one event's hooks run in one ordered chain per subject, end when the tab closes or moves to another document, and pause and resume by one rule. A hook reaches only the tabs its filter covers, and a rename from code applies only to the document it was made for.
